Getting Started

Introduction to Python

A high-level, interpreted, dynamically typed language that favours readability — the most common language for interviews, scripting, data and AI.

Python — a general-purpose language where code is compiled to bytecode and run by a virtual machine (CPython). You write less code per idea than in C++ or Java, at the cost of raw speed.

Why Python?

Readable

Indentation is syntax, so code looks like pseudocode. Ideal for interviews — the interviewer reads your logic, not boilerplate.

Batteries included

A huge standard library: collections, itertools, heapq, bisect, functools, json, re, pathlib…

Dynamic typing

Types belong to values, not variables. A name can point to an int now and a str later.

Everywhere

Web back ends (Django, FastAPI), automation, data science (pandas, NumPy), ML (PyTorch), scripting.

How Python runs your code
1
Source
You write app.py — plain text.
2
Compile to bytecode
CPython parses it and compiles it to bytecode (cached as __pycache__/*.pyc).
3
Run on the PVM
The Python Virtual Machine executes the bytecode instruction by instruction — that is the “interpreted” part.
ImplementationWhat it is
CPythonThe reference implementation, written in C. What you get from python.org. Has the GIL.
PyPyJIT-compiled — often several times faster for long-running pure-Python code.
Jython / IronPythonPython on the JVM / .NET (rarely used today).
Your first program
Python
name = "Python"
version = 3
print(f"Hello, {name} {version}!")   # Hello, Python 3!
print(type(name), type(version))    # <class 'str'> <class 'int'>
Terminal
$ python3 --version          # check the installed version
$ python3 app.py             # run a file
$ python3                    # interactive REPL (exit with Ctrl-D)
$ python3 -m venv .venv      # create an isolated environment
Style: PEP 8 and the Zen
  • 4 spaces per indent level, never tabs.
  • snake_case for variables and functions, PascalCase for classes, UPPER_CASE for constants.
  • Lines around 79–99 characters; one statement per line.
  • Run import this to read The Zen of Python — “Readability counts.”, “Explicit is better than implicit.”
Version

Python 2 reached end-of-life in 2020. Everything in these notes is Python 3 (3.10+ for match).

Getting Started

Syntax, Comments & Keywords

The rules of how Python code is written: indentation instead of braces, comments and docstrings, how lines continue, what you may name things, and the reserved keywords.

Indentation is the block structure

Where C and Java use { }, Python uses a colon : followed by an indented block. Every line in a block must use the same indentation — the convention is 4 spaces.

Python
def classify(n):
    if n < 0:
        return "negative"        # inside the if-block
    elif n == 0:
        return "zero"
    return "positive"            # back at the function-block level

print(classify(-3), classify(0), classify(7))   # negative zero positive
Python
src = "def f():\nreturn 1\n"          # body not indented
try:
    compile(src, "<demo>", "exec")
except IndentationError as e:
    print(type(e).__name__, "-", e.msg)   # IndentationError - expected an indented block after function definition on line 1
Tabs vs spaces

Mixing tabs and spaces raises TabError in Python 3. Configure your editor to insert 4 spaces when you press Tab.

Comments & docstrings

# comment

Ignored by the interpreter. Explains why the code does something — the code already says what.

Docstring

A string literal as the first statement of a module, class or function. Stored in __doc__, shown by help() and IDEs.

Python
def area(r):
    """Return the area of a circle of radius r.

    Uses 3.14159 for pi; pass a float or int.
    """
    # the formula is pi * r squared
    return 3.14159 * r * r

print(area.__doc__.splitlines()[0])   # Return the area of a circle of radius r.
print(round(area(2), 2))              # 12.57
Key point

Python has no block-comment syntax. Use several # lines. A bare triple-quoted string in the middle of code is evaluated and thrown away — it works as a comment but is not one.

Statements vs expressions

Expression — anything that produces a value: 2 + 3, len(xs), x if c else y, lambda n: n * 2. Expressions can appear inside other expressions.

Statement — an instruction that does something: x = 5, if, for, def, import, return. A statement cannot be used where a value is expected.

Python
x = 5                                   # assignment statement
y = (x * 2) + 1                         # the right side is an expression
print(y, (z := x + 1), z)               # 11 6 6   ← := is an assignment *expression*
print(eval("x * 10"))                   # 50       ← eval evaluates one expression
exec("w = x + 100")                     # exec runs statements
print(w)                                # 105
Key point

That's why lambda bodies must be a single expression (no =, no for), and why if x = 5: is a syntax error — assignment is a statement.

Long lines & multiple statements
Python
total = (1 + 2 +
         3 + 4)                # implicit continuation inside ( ) [ ] { }
fruits = [
    "apple",
    "banana",                  # a trailing comma is allowed — cleaner diffs
]
legacy = 1 + \
         2                     # explicit backslash continuation (avoid)
a = 1; b = 2                   # ; separates statements (discouraged)
print(total, len(fruits), legacy, a + b)   # 10 2 3 3
Do

Wrap long expressions in parentheses and break after an operator or comma.

Avoid

Backslash continuations — a single trailing space after \ becomes a syntax error.

Names (identifiers)
Rule / conventionExample
Letters, digits, _; can't start with a digittotal_2 ✓ · 2total ✗
Case-sensitivecount, Count and COUNT are three names
Can't be a keywordclass = 1 ✗ → class_ = 1 ✓
Unicode letters allowedπ = 3.14159 works (but stick to ASCII in shared code)
snake_casevariables, functions, modules
PascalCaseclasses
UPPER_CASEconstants (by convention only — nothing stops reassignment)
_leadinginternal / private by convention
__dunder__reserved for Python's special names
_a throwaway value: for _ in range(3); in the REPL it's the last result
Python
print("my_var".isidentifier(), "2cool".isidentifier())   # True False
π = 3.14159
print(round(2 * π, 2))                                    # 6.28
Watch out

Don't shadow built-ins: list = [1, 2] or id = 5 hides the built-in list() / id() for the rest of the scope, leading to confusing TypeError: 'list' object is not callable.

Keywords & soft keywords

Keywords are reserved words you can't use as names. Soft keywords (match, case, type, _) are keywords only in specific positions, so old code using match as a variable still works.

Python
import keyword
print(len(keyword.kwlist))                                     # 35
print(keyword.iskeyword("class"), keyword.iskeyword("match"))  # True False
print(keyword.softkwlist)                                      # ['_', 'case', 'match', 'type']
match = "still a valid variable name"
print(match.split()[0])                                        # still
PurposeKeywords
ValuesTrue False None
Logic & testsand or not in is
Branchingif elif else
Loopsfor while break continue
Functionsdef return lambda yield global nonlocal
Classes & modulesclass import from as
Errors & resourcestry except finally raise assert with
Asyncasync await
Miscpass del
Placeholders: pass and ...
Python
def todo():
    pass                  # a statement that does nothing

class Config: ...         # Ellipsis — a common "fill in later" body

print(todo(), Config.__name__, ...)   # None Config Ellipsis
Exploring from the REPL: help, dir, type
Python
print(type(3.0).__name__)                             # float
print([n for n in dir(str) if n.startswith("st")])    # ['startswith', 'strip']
print(len.__doc__.splitlines()[0])                    # Return the number of items in a container.
REPL
>>> help(str.split)       # full documentation (q to quit)
>>> dir([])               # every attribute/method of a list
>>> 2 + 3
5
>>> _ * 10                # _ holds the last REPL result
50
Why does Python use indentation instead of braces?

To make the visual structure be the actual structure — code that looks nested is nested. It removes a whole class of bugs (misleading indentation) and style debates, and keeps code readable.

What is the difference between a comment and a docstring?

A comment (#) is discarded by the parser. A docstring is a real string object stored in the __doc__ attribute of a module, class or function, so help(), IDEs and documentation tools can read it at runtime.

Is match a keyword?

It's a soft keyword: it acts as a keyword only at the start of a match statement, so match = 5 is still a legal assignment. The same applies to case, type and _.

Getting Started

Variables, Types & Operators

A variable is a name bound to an object. Every object has an identity, a type and a value.

Name binding — x = 10 creates an int object and binds the name x to it. Assignment never copies — it only attaches another name to the same object.

Built-in types
TypeExampleMutable?
int42, -7, 10**100 (unlimited size)No
float3.14, 1e-9 (64-bit IEEE 754)No
complex2 + 3jNo
boolTrue, False (a subclass of int)No
str'hi', "hi", '''multi'''No
NoneTypeNone — the single “no value” objectNo
list / dict / set[1, 2], {'a': 1}, {1, 2}Yes
tuple / frozenset(1, 2), frozenset({1})No
Python
x = 10
print(type(x))                    # <class 'int'>
x = "now a string"                # same name, different object and type
print(isinstance(x, str))         # True
print(True + True)                # 2
Operators at a glance

Arithmetic + - * / // % **, comparison == != < <= > >= (chainable), logical and or not, identity is, membership in, bitwise & | ^ ~ << >> and the walrus :=. The next topic, Operators & Precedence, covers each one in depth.

Python
print(7 / 2, 7 // 2, 7 % 2, -7 // 2)   # 3.5 3 1 -4
print(2 ** 10, divmod(17, 5))          # 1024 (3, 2)
print(0 < 5 < 10)                      # True
print("" or "default", 0 and 99)       # default 0
if (n := len("hello")) > 3:
    print(n)                           # 5
== vs is

== — equality

Calls __eq__: do the two objects have the same value?

is — identity

Are they the same object in memory (same id)? Use only for singletons: None, True, False.

Python
a = [1, 2, 3]
b = [1, 2, 3]
c = a
print(a == b, a is b, a is c)   # True False True
Watch out

CPython caches small integers (−5…256) and some strings, so a is b may be True for them by accident. Never rely on it — compare values with ==.

Truthiness

In a condition, every object is truthy except these falsy values:

FalseNone0, 0.0, 0j''[](){}set()range(0)
Python
items = []
if not items:              # idiomatic emptiness check
    print("empty")         # empty
print(bool("0"), bool(0))  # True False
Type conversion
Python
print(int("42") + 1, float("2.5"), str(99) + "!")   # 43 2.5 99!
print(int(3.99), round(2.5), round(3.5))            # 3 2 4
print(int("ff", 16), bin(10), hex(255))             # 255 0b1010 0xff
Key point

round() uses banker's rounding: halves go to the nearest even number, so round(2.5) == 2.

Getting Started

Operators & Precedence

Every operator Python has, how tightly each one binds, and the surprising corners — floor division with negatives, and/or returning operands, chained comparisons and bit tricks.

Precedence table (highest first)
LevelOperatorsNotes
1(…) […] {…}, x[i], x(…), x.attrGrouping, subscription, calls, attribute access
2await xInside async def
3**Power — right-associative; binds tighter than a unary minus on its left
4+x -x ~xUnary plus, minus, bitwise NOT
5* @ / // %Multiplicative (@ is matrix multiplication)
6+ -Additive
7<< >>Shifts
8&Bitwise AND
9^Bitwise XOR
10|Bitwise OR
11in not in is is not < <= > >= != ==All comparisons share one level and chain
12not xBoolean NOT
13andShort-circuit AND
14orShort-circuit OR
15x if c else yConditional expression
16lambdaAnonymous function
17:=Assignment expression (walrus) — lowest
Key point

Everything is left-to-right associative except ** (right-to-left) and comparisons (which chain). When in doubt, add parentheses — they cost nothing and help the reader.

Arithmetic
Python
print(7 / 2, 7 // 2, 7 % 2)           # 3.5 3 1
print(-7 // 2, -7 % 2)                # -4 1
print(7 // -2, 7 % -2)                # -4 -1
print(divmod(-7, 2))                  # (-4, 1)
print(7.5 // 2, 7.5 % 2)              # 3.0 1.5
print(2 ** 3 ** 2, (2 ** 3) ** 2)     # 512 64
print(-2 ** 2, (-2) ** 2)             # -4 4
print(2 ** -1, 10 ** 20)              # 0.5 100000000000000000000
OperatorMeaningWatch out
/True divisionAlways returns a float: 4 / 2 == 2.0
//Floor divisionRounds toward −∞, not toward 0: -7 // 2 == -4 (C/Java give -3)
%ModuloResult has the sign of the divisor: -7 % 2 == 1; always (a // b) * b + a % b == a
**PowerRight-associative; pow(a, b, m) does fast modular exponentiation
divmod(a, b)(a // b, a % b) at onceHandy for digit extraction and time conversions
Key point

Python's % makes wrap-around indexing easy: (i - 1) % n is always a valid index, even when i == 0. For C-style truncation use int(a / b) or math.fmod.

Comparisons chain
Python
x = 5
print(1 < x < 10, 10 > x >= 5)        # True True
print(1 < 2 == 2)                     # True   ← means (1 < 2) and (2 == 2)
print((1 < 2) == 2)                   # False  ← True == 2 is False
print(False == False in [False])      # True   ← (False == False) and (False in [False])
print("apple" < "banana" < "cherry")  # True   ← strings compare lexicographically
print([1, 2] < [1, 3], (2, 0) > (1, 9))   # True True

a < b < c evaluates b only once and means a < b and b < c. Sequences compare element by element, which is why tuples make great multi-key sort keys.

and, or, not — short-circuit and return operands
Python
print(0 or "default", "x" or "default")   # default x
print(0 and 1 / 0, 3 and 4)               # 0 4   ← 1 / 0 is never evaluated
print([] or None or 0)                    # 0     ← every operand falsy → the last one
print(not "", not "hi")                   # True False

name = ""
print(name or "Anonymous")                # Anonymous
ExpressionReturns
a or ba if a is truthy, otherwise b
a and ba if a is falsy, otherwise b
not aAlways a real bool
Watch out

count = user_count or 10 replaces a legitimate 0 with 10. When 0, '' or [] are valid values, test explicitly: 10 if user_count is None else user_count.

Bitwise operators & tricks
Python
a, b = 0b1100, 0b1010                 # 12, 10
print(a & b, a | b, a ^ b)            # 8 14 6
print(bin(a & b), ~a)                 # 0b1000 -13   ← ~x == -x - 1
print(1 << 4, 100 >> 2)               # 16 25        ← x << k == x * 2**k
Python
def is_power_of_two(x):
    return x > 0 and x & (x - 1) == 0     # clears the lowest set bit

print(is_power_of_two(64), is_power_of_two(96))   # True False
print(40 & -40)                         # 8      ← lowest set bit of 0b101000
print((13 >> 2) & 1)                    # 1      ← bit 2 of 0b1101
print((255).bit_count(), (255).bit_length())   # 8 8

READ, WRITE, EXEC = 1, 2, 4             # flags packed in one int
perms = READ | WRITE
perms &= ~WRITE                         # clear a flag
perms |= EXEC                           # set a flag
print(perms, bool(perms & READ), bool(perms & WRITE))   # 5 True False

nums = [4, 1, 2, 1, 2]
single = 0
for n in nums:
    single ^= n                         # x ^ x == 0, so pairs cancel out
print(single)                           # 4
Key point

Unlike C, bitwise operators bind tighter than comparisons in Python: n & 1 == 0 means (n & 1) == 0. Python ints are unbounded, so ~ and negative numbers behave as if they had infinitely many leading 1 bits.

Augmented assignment

x += 1, -=, *=, /=, //=, %=, **=, &=, |=, ^=, <<=, >>=, @=. For mutable objects they modify in place (__iadd__); for immutable ones they build a new object and rebind the name.

Python
a = [1]
b = a
a += [2]              # list.__iadd__ → extends the same list
print(b)              # [1, 2]
a = a + [3]           # + builds a NEW list; b still sees the old one
print(a, b)           # [1, 2, 3] [1, 2]
Watch out

There is no ++ or --. i++ is a syntax error and ++i quietly means +(+i) — it does nothing.

Conditional expression & walrus
Python
age = 20
print("adult" if age >= 18 else "minor")          # adult

data = [4, 9, 1, 7]
if (n := len(data)) > 3:                          # assign and test in one step
    print(f"{n} items")                           # 4 items
big_squares = [y for x in data if (y := x * x) > 10]
print(big_squares)                                # [16, 81, 49]
Membership, identity & overloading
Python
print("ell" in "hello", 3 in {1, 2, 3}, "a" in {"a": 1})   # True True True
a, b = [], []
print(a == b, a is b, a is not b)                         # True False True
print("ab" * 3, [0] * 3, [1] + [2])                       # ababab [0, 0, 0] [1, 2]

import operator
print(operator.add(2, 3), operator.mul("ha", 2))          # 5 haha

Operators are method calls in disguise: a + b → a.__add__(b), a < b → a.__lt__(b), x in c → c.__contains__(x). That's how str, list and your own classes give + and * their own meaning (see Magic (Dunder) Methods). The operator module exposes each one as a function for sorted(key=…), reduce and map.

Common pitfalls
Python
x = 5
print(x == 1 or 2)            # 2      ← parsed as (x == 1) or 2 → always truthy!
print(x in (1, 2))            # False  ← what was meant
print(not x == 5, x != 5)     # False False   ← not binds looser than ==
print(-3 ** 2)                # -9     ← -(3 ** 2)
print(0.1 + 0.2 == 0.3)       # False  ← floats; compare with math.isclose
print(1 + 2 * 3, (1 + 2) * 3) # 7 9
What do and and or return?

They return one of their operands, not necessarily a bool. a or b returns a if it's truthy, else b; a and b returns a if it's falsy, else b. Evaluation stops as soon as the result is known (short-circuit), so the right side may never run.

Why is -7 // 2 equal to -4 in Python?

// is floor division: it rounds toward negative infinity. −3.5 floors to −4. Correspondingly -7 % 2 == 1, keeping the identity (a // b) * b + a % b == a. C and Java truncate toward zero instead (−3 and −1).

How do you check whether a number is a power of two?

n > 0 and n & (n - 1) == 0. Subtracting 1 flips the lowest set bit and everything below it, so ANDing clears that bit — the result is 0 only if there was exactly one set bit.

What does 1 < 2 == 2 evaluate to?

True. Comparisons chain: it means 1 < 2 and 2 == 2. It is not (1 < 2) == 2, which would be True == 2 → False.

Getting Started

Input & Output

Talking to the console: everything print() can do, reading typed input with input(), and the fast sys.stdin patterns used in coding rounds.

print() in full

Signature: print(*objects, sep=' ', end='\n', file=sys.stdout, flush=False). Each object is converted with str(), joined by sep, followed by end.

Python
print("a", "b", "c")                      # a b c
print("a", "b", "c", sep="-")             # a-b-c
print(2026, 9, 29, sep="/")               # 2026/9/29
print("Loading", end=""); print("...done")   # Loading...done
print(*[1, 2, 3], sep=", ")               # 1, 2, 3
print("tab\tseparated", repr("hi"))      # tab	separated 'hi'
ParameterDefaultUse it for
sep' 'What goes between the objects
end'\n'What goes after the last object — end='' keeps the cursor on the line
filesys.stdoutAny object with .write(): sys.stderr, an open file, an io.StringIO
flushFalseForce the output out immediately (progress bars, logs piped to another program)
Python
import io
buf = io.StringIO()
# file= sends the text to buf instead of the screen:
print("to a buffer", 42, file=buf)
print(repr(buf.getvalue()))           # 'to a buffer 42\n'
result = print("shown", end=" ")
print(result)                         # shown None
Key point

print returns None — it only has a side effect. Errors and diagnostics belong on sys.stderr (print(msg, file=sys.stderr)) so they don't mix with the program's real output.

input() — reading a line

input(prompt) shows the prompt, waits for the user to press Enter and returns the line as a str, without the newline. Convert it yourself.

greet.py
name = input("Your name? ")
age = int(input("Your age? "))
print(f"Hi {name}, next year you'll be {age + 1}")

$ python3 greet.py
Your name? Asha
Your age? 24
Hi Asha, next year you'll be 25

Below, sys.stdin is swapped for an in-memory stream so the example runs without a keyboard — exactly how tests feed input to a program.

Python
import io, sys
fake = io.StringIO("Asha\n3 1 4 1 5\n42\n")
old, sys.stdin = sys.stdin, fake          # pretend the user typed three lines
try:
    name = input()                        # 'Asha' — always a str
    nums = list(map(int, input().split()))
    n = int(input())
    try:
        input()                           # nothing left to read
    except EOFError:
        eof = True
finally:
    sys.stdin = old                       # always restore the real stdin
print(name, nums, n + 1, eof)             # Asha [3, 1, 4, 1, 5] 43 True
Watch out

input() never returns a number: input() + 1 is a TypeError. And at end of input (Ctrl-D, or a judge's file running out) it raises EOFError.

Parsing patterns
Input shapeCode
One integern = int(input())
Two integers on a linea, b = map(int, input().split())
A list of integersnums = list(map(int, input().split()))
n lines of textlines = [input() for _ in range(n)]
A grid of charactersgrid = [list(input()) for _ in range(rows)]
Comma-separatedparts = input().split(',')
Everything at oncetokens = sys.stdin.read().split()
Fast input for coding rounds

input() is slow for 10⁵+ lines. sys.stdin.readline is much faster, but it keeps the trailing \n — strip it (or let int() / split() ignore it).

Python
import io
data = io.StringIO("3\n10 20\n30 40\n50 60\n")
readline = data.readline                  # real code: import sys; readline = sys.stdin.readline
n = int(readline())                       # int() ignores the trailing newline
pairs = [tuple(map(int, readline().split())) for _ in range(n)]
print(pairs)                              # [(10, 20), (30, 40), (50, 60)]
print(repr(io.StringIO("hi\n").readline()))   # 'hi\n'

tokens = iter(io.StringIO("2\n5 7\n").read().split())   # read everything, walk token by token
count = int(next(tokens))
print(sum(int(next(tokens)) for _ in range(count)))    # 12
Fast output
Python
import sys
written = sys.stdout.write("written ")    # no automatic newline; returns chars written
print(written)                            # written 8

results = [3, 1, 4]
print(" ".join(map(str, results)))        # 3 1 4
Do

Collect answers in a list and print once with '\n'.join(map(str, answers)).

Avoid

Calling print a million times in a loop — each call has overhead (and may flush).

Pretty-printing nested data
Python
from pprint import pprint
data = {"name": "Asha", "skills": ["python", "sql", "dsa"],
        "address": {"city": "Pune", "pin": 411001}}
pprint(data, width=40)
Output
{'address': {'city': 'Pune',
             'pin': 411001},
 'name': 'Asha',
 'skills': ['python', 'sql', 'dsa']}
Key point

pprint wraps long structures and sorts dict keys (pass sort_dicts=False to keep insertion order). pprint.pformat returns the text instead of printing it.

Formatting output
Python
price, qty, item = 49.5, 3, "pen"
print(f"{item:<6}|{qty:>3}|{price * qty:8.2f}|")   # pen   |  3|  148.50|
print(f"{0.4567:.1%}  {1234567:,}  {7:03d}")       # 45.7%  1,234,567  007

f-strings and the format-spec mini-language (:<6, .2f, ,, %) are covered in depth in String Formatting under Strings & Numbers.

What type does input() return?

Always str (without the trailing newline), even if the user types digits. Convert explicitly: int(input()), float(input()), or map(int, input().split()) for several numbers.

Why use sys.stdin.readline instead of input()?

It avoids input()'s per-call overhead (prompt handling, newline stripping), which matters with hundreds of thousands of lines. Remember it keeps the \n, and returns '' (not EOFError) at end of input.

What is the difference between print and sys.stdout.write?

print accepts any objects, converts them with str(), adds separators and a newline, and returns None. sys.stdout.write accepts one string, adds nothing, and returns the number of characters written.

Getting Started

Control Flow

if/elif/else, for, while, match — plus a few Python-only twists like for … else.

Conditionals
Python
def grade(score):
    if score >= 90:
        return "A"
    elif score >= 75:
        return "B"
    else:
        return "C"

print(grade(95), grade(80), grade(40))       # A B C
status = "adult" if 20 >= 18 else "minor"     # conditional expression
print(status)                                  # adult
Loops

for iterates over any iterable (lists, strings, dicts, files, generators). while repeats while a condition is truthy.

Python
for i in range(2, 10, 3):          # start, stop (exclusive), step
    print(i, end=" ")               # 2 5 8
print()
for i, ch in enumerate("abc", start=1):
    print(i, ch, end=" | ")         # 1 a | 2 b | 3 c |
print()
for name, age in zip(["a", "b"], [20, 30]):
    print(name, age, end="; ")      # a 20; b 30;
HelperGives youInstead of
enumerate(it)(index, item) pairsfor i in range(len(xs))
zip(a, b)Items side by side (stops at the shortest)Manual index juggling
reversed(seq)Items back to front, lazilyseq[::-1] (copies)
sorted(it, key=…)A new sorted listSorting then copying
Key point

More patterns — while … else, breaking out of nested loops, sentinel loops, modifying while iterating — are in Loops & Iteration Patterns.

break, continue and loop else

A loop's else block runs only if the loop finished without break — perfect for search loops.

Python
def find_first_negative(nums):
    for i, n in enumerate(nums):
        if n < 0:
            break
    else:
        return None          # no break → nothing found
    return i

print(find_first_negative([3, 1, -2, 5]))   # 2
print(find_first_negative([1, 2]))          # None
match — structural pattern matching (3.10+)
Python
def describe(cmd):
    match cmd:
        case ["go", direction]:
            return f"moving {direction}"
        case ["pick", *items] if items:
            return f"picking {len(items)} items"
        case {"action": "quit"}:
            return "bye"
        case int() | float() as n:
            return f"number {n}"
        case _:
            return "unknown"

print(describe(["go", "north"]))       # moving north
print(describe(["pick", "a", "b"]))    # picking 2 items
print(describe({"action": "quit"}))    # bye
print(describe(3.5))                   # number 3.5
Key point

match is not a C switch: it destructures sequences, mappings and objects, binds names, and supports guards (if). See Pattern Matching (match) for every pattern kind and its pitfalls.

No do … while, no ++
Do

Use while True: with a break at the end for do-while logic, and i += 1 to increment.

Avoid

Writing i++ (a syntax error) or ++i (parsed as +(+i) — silently does nothing).

Getting Started

Loops & Iteration Patterns

Python's for walks over any iterable — there is no C-style for (i = 0; …). This topic collects the loop patterns, helpers and pitfalls you meet every day.

range in detail
Python
print(list(range(5)))                 # [0, 1, 2, 3, 4]
print(list(range(2, 12, 3)))          # [2, 5, 8, 11]
print(list(range(5, 0, -1)))          # [5, 4, 3, 2, 1]
print(list(range(3, 0)))              # []   ← empty: a negative step is needed to count down
r = range(0, 10**12, 2)               # no list is built — O(1) memory
print(len(r), r[10], r[-1])           # 500000000000 20 999999999998
print(999_999 in r, 1_000 in r)       # False True   ← O(1) arithmetic check
GoalLoop header
0 … n−1for i in range(n)
1 … n inclusivefor i in range(1, n + 1)
n−1 down to 0for i in range(n - 1, -1, -1) or reversed(range(n))
Every other indexfor i in range(0, n, 2)
Items and their indexfor i, x in enumerate(xs)
Two lists in stepfor a, b in zip(xs, ys)
Neighbouring pairsfor a, b in zip(xs, xs[1:]) or itertools.pairwise(xs)
while loops and while … else

Use while when you don't know the number of iterations in advance. Like for, it takes an else that runs only if the loop ended without break.

Python
n, steps = 27, 0
while n != 1:                         # Collatz sequence
    n = n // 2 if n % 2 == 0 else 3 * n + 1
    steps += 1
print(steps)                          # 111

def is_prime(n):
    if n < 2:
        return False
    d = 2
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            break                     # found a divisor
        d += 1
    else:
        return True                   # no break → no divisor found
    return False

print([k for k in range(20) if is_prime(k)])   # [2, 3, 5, 7, 11, 13, 17, 19]
Python
attempts = 0
while True:                           # the do-while idiom: body runs at least once
    attempts += 1
    if attempts * attempts > 20:
        break
print(attempts)                       # 5
Breaking out of nested loops

break only leaves the innermost loop. Three ways out of two:

Python
grid = [[1, 2, 3], [4, 5, 6], [7, 8, 9]]

# 1. A flag checked by the outer loop
found = None
for r, row in enumerate(grid):
    for c, val in enumerate(row):
        if val == 5:
            found = (r, c)
            break
    if found:
        break
print(found)                          # (1, 1)

# 2. Put the loops in a function and return (usually the cleanest)
def find(grid, target):
    for r, row in enumerate(grid):
        for c, val in enumerate(row):
            if val == target:
                return r, c
    return None
print(find(grid, 8), find(grid, 0))   # (2, 1) None

# 3. One flat loop over all coordinates
from itertools import product
hit = next(((r, c) for r, c in product(range(3), range(3)) if grid[r][c] == 9), None)
print(hit)                            # (2, 2)
Iterating dicts, in order, in reverse, in parallel
Python
prices = {"tea": 20, "coffee": 35, "juice": 50}
print([k for k in prices])                        # ['tea', 'coffee', 'juice']
print([f"{k}={v}" for k, v in prices.items()])    # ['tea=20', 'coffee=35', 'juice=50']
print(sorted(prices, key=prices.get, reverse=True))   # ['juice', 'coffee', 'tea']

names = ["asha", "ravi", "kiran"]
scores = [91, 84, 77]
print(list(enumerate(names, start=1)))    # [(1, 'asha'), (2, 'ravi'), (3, 'kiran')]
print(dict(zip(names, scores)))           # {'asha': 91, 'ravi': 84, 'kiran': 77}
print(list(zip(names, [1, 2])))           # [('asha', 1), ('ravi', 2)]   ← stops at the shortest
try:
    list(zip(names, [1, 2], strict=True)) # 3.10+: insist on equal lengths
except ValueError as e:
    print(e)                              # zip() argument 2 is shorter than argument 1
print(list(reversed(names)))              # ['kiran', 'ravi', 'asha']

pairs = [("a", 1), ("b", 2)]
letters, numbers = zip(*pairs)            # "unzip"
print(letters, numbers)                   # ('a', 'b') (1, 2)
Don't modify a collection while looping over it
Python
nums = [1, 2, 2, 3, 4]
for x in nums:
    if x % 2 == 0:
        nums.remove(x)        # shifts later items left → the loop skips one
print(nums)                   # [1, 2, 3]   ← a 2 survived!

nums = [1, 2, 2, 3, 4]
print([x for x in nums if x % 2])       # [1, 3]   ← build a new list instead

d = {"a": 1, "b": 2}
try:
    for k in d:
        if d[k] == 1:
            del d[k]
except RuntimeError as e:
    print(e)                  # dictionary changed size during iteration
d = {k: v for k, v in d.items() if v != 1}
print(d)                      # {'b': 2}

Build a new collection

A comprehension that keeps what you want — the idiomatic fix.

Loop over a copy

for x in nums[:]: or for k in list(d): — then mutating the original is safe.

Loop backwards by index

for i in range(len(xs) - 1, -1, -1): del xs[i] — deletions don't shift unvisited items.

Sentinel loops & iter(callable, sentinel)
Python
import io
from functools import partial

stream = io.StringIO("alpha\nbeta\n\ngamma\n")
lines = []
while (line := stream.readline().rstrip("\n")):   # stop at the first empty line
    lines.append(line)
print(lines)                                      # ['alpha', 'beta']

f = io.StringIO("abcdefgh")
chunks = list(iter(partial(f.read, 3), ""))       # call f.read(3) until it returns ""
print(chunks)                                     # ['abc', 'def', 'gh']
Key point

The two-argument iter(func, sentinel) calls func() repeatedly and stops when it returns sentinel — perfect for reading fixed-size blocks from files and sockets.

Loop variables & scope
Python
for i in range(3):
    pass
print(i)                      # 2   ← the loop variable survives the loop

squares = [j * j for j in range(3)]
print("j" in dir())           # False   ← comprehension variables don't leak
Watch out

If the loop body never runs (empty iterable), the loop variable is never assigned — using it afterwards raises NameError.

Faster loops
SlowerFasterWhy
total = 0 + for x in xs: total += xsum(xs)Built-ins loop in C
Loop + appendList comprehensionNo repeated .append lookup and call
if x in big_list inside a loopConvert to a set onceO(1) instead of O(n) per check
for i in range(len(xs)): xs[i]for x in xsNo indexing per step
s += piece in a loop''.join(pieces)Avoids O(n²) copying
obj.method looked up each timem = obj.method before the loopOne attribute lookup instead of n
When would you use for … else?

For searches: the else runs only when the loop completed without break, i.e. when nothing was found. It replaces a separate found = False flag.

How do you iterate over two lists at the same time?

for a, b in zip(xs, ys). zip stops at the shortest input; use zip(..., strict=True) (3.10+) to raise on a length mismatch, or itertools.zip_longest to pad the shorter one.

Why is removing items from a list while iterating over it a bug?

The list iterator walks by index. Removing an item shifts the rest left, so the item right after the removed one is skipped. Iterate over a copy, iterate backwards, or build a new list with a comprehension.

Getting Started

Pattern Matching (match)

match (Python 3.10+) compares a value against a series of patterns — shapes, not just values — and binds the pieces it finds to names.

Structural pattern matching — match subject: followed by case pattern: blocks. Cases are tried top to bottom; the first one that matches runs, and there is no fall-through.

Literals, OR-patterns, as and the wildcard
Python
def http_status(code):
    match code:
        case 200 | 201 | 204:            # OR-pattern
            return "success"
        case 301 | 302 as r:             # bind the matched value with as
            return f"redirect {r}"
        case 404:
            return "not found"
        case _:                          # wildcard: matches anything, binds nothing
            return "other"

print(http_status(201), http_status(302))   # success redirect 302
print(http_status(404), http_status(500))   # not found other
Sequence patterns
Python
def summarize(seq):
    match seq:
        case []:
            return "empty"
        case [x]:
            return f"one item: {x}"
        case [x, y]:
            return f"pair: {x}, {y}"
        case [first, *rest]:             # star captures the remainder as a list
            return f"{first} and {len(rest)} more"

print(summarize([]))              # empty
print(summarize([7]))             # one item: 7
print(summarize((1, 2)))          # pair: 1, 2   ← tuples match list patterns too
print(summarize([1, 2, 3, 4]))    # 1 and 3 more
print(summarize("ab"))            # None   ← str is NOT treated as a sequence here
Python
def fizzbuzz(n):
    match (n % 3, n % 5):                # match a tuple built on the spot
        case (0, 0): return "FizzBuzz"
        case (0, _): return "Fizz"
        case (_, 0): return "Buzz"
        case _: return str(n)

print(" ".join(fizzbuzz(i) for i in range(1, 16)))   # 1 2 Fizz 4 Buzz Fizz 7 8 Fizz Buzz 11 Fizz 13 14 FizzBuzz
Mapping patterns
Python
def handle(event):
    match event:
        case {"type": "click", "x": x, "y": y}:
            return f"click at ({x}, {y})"
        case {"type": "key", "key": str(k)} if len(k) == 1:
            return f"key {k!r}"
        case {"type": "resize", **rest}:
            return f"resize {rest}"
        case {"type": t}:
            return f"unhandled {t}"

print(handle({"type": "click", "x": 3, "y": 4, "ts": 99}))   # click at (3, 4)   ← extra keys are ignored
print(handle({"type": "key", "key": "q"}))                  # key 'q'
print(handle({"type": "resize", "w": 800, "h": 600}))       # resize {'w': 800, 'h': 600}
print(handle({"type": "scroll"}))                           # unhandled scroll
Key point

Mapping patterns match if the listed keys are present — other keys are allowed. Sequence patterns are the opposite: the length must match exactly unless you use *rest.

Class patterns
Python
from dataclasses import dataclass

@dataclass
class Point:
    x: int
    y: int

@dataclass
class Circle:
    center: Point
    r: float

def where(shape):
    match shape:
        case Point(x=0, y=0):
            return "origin"
        case Point(0, y):                # positional → uses Point.__match_args__
            return f"on the y-axis at {y}"
        case Point(x, y) if x == y:      # guard
            return "on the diagonal"
        case Circle(Point(0, 0), r):     # patterns nest
            return f"circle of radius {r} at the origin"
        case Point() | Circle():         # just an isinstance check
            return "somewhere else"
        case _:
            return "not a shape"

print(where(Point(0, 0)), "|", where(Point(0, 5)))        # origin | on the y-axis at 5
print(where(Point(3, 3)), "|", where(Circle(Point(0, 0), 2)))   # on the diagonal | circle of radius 2 at the origin
print(where(Point(1, 2)), "|", where("hi"))               # somewhere else | not a shape
print(Point.__match_args__)                               # ('x', 'y')
Python
def kind(value):
    match value:
        case bool():                     # check bool before int — bool is a subclass of int
            return "bool"
        case int(n) if n < 0:            # built-ins match the whole value positionally
            return "negative int"
        case int() | float():
            return "number"
        case str() as s:
            return f"string of {len(s)}"
        case _:
            return type(value).__name__

print(kind(True), kind(-4), kind(2.5), kind("abc"), kind(None))   # bool negative int number string of 3 NoneType

A class pattern Cls(...) first checks isinstance(subject, Cls), then matches attributes: keyword sub-patterns by name, positional ones via the class's __match_args__ tuple (generated automatically by @dataclass and namedtuple).

Guards

case pattern if condition: — the pattern must match and the condition must be truthy. Names bound by the pattern are available in the guard.

Python
def classify(pair):
    match pair:
        case (a, b) if a == b:
            return "equal"
        case (a, b) if a > b:
            return "descending"
        case (_, _):
            return "ascending"

print(classify((2, 2)), classify((5, 1)), classify((1, 5)))   # equal descending ascending
The capture-pattern trap

A bare name in a pattern is a capture: it matches anything and assigns to that name. To compare against a constant, use a dotted name (a value pattern) such as Color.RED or http.HTTPStatus.OK.

Python
RED = "red"

def is_red(color):
    match color:
        case RED:                        # captures! binds a local RED = color
            return True
    return False

print(is_red("blue"))                    # True   ← oops

from enum import Enum
class Color(Enum):
    RED = "red"
    BLUE = "blue"

def is_red_fixed(color):
    match color:
        case Color.RED:                  # dotted name → compared with ==
            return True
        case _:
            return False

print(is_red_fixed(Color.BLUE), is_red_fixed(Color.RED))   # False True
Python
src = "match x:\n    case y:\n        pass\n    case 1:\n        pass\n"
try:
    compile(src, "<demo>", "exec")
except SyntaxError as e:
    print(e.msg)          # name capture 'y' makes remaining patterns unreachable
Pattern kinds cheat sheet
PatternExampleMatches
Literalcase 0: case "quit": case None:Equal value (None/True/False compared with is)
Capturecase x:Anything; binds x
Wildcardcase _:Anything; binds nothing
Valuecase Color.RED:Equal to a dotted constant
ORcase 1 | 2 | 3:Any alternative
AScase [x, y] as pair:The inner pattern; binds the whole subject too
Sequencecase [x, *rest]:Lists/tuples (not str/bytes) of the right length
Mappingcase {"id": i}:Dicts containing those keys; **rest collects the others
Classcase Point(x, y=0):isinstance + attribute patterns
When to use match
Do

Use match to branch on the shape of data: parsed commands, JSON events, AST nodes, tuples of states.

Avoid

Using it as a glorified switch for simple value → value mapping — a dict lookup is shorter and faster.

How is match different from a C/Java switch?

switch compares one value against constants. match tests structure: it can destructure lists, dicts and objects, bind variables, combine alternatives with |, and filter with guards. There is no fall-through and no break — the first matching case runs and the statement ends.

Why does case RED: match every value?

A bare name is a capture pattern: it always matches and binds the subject to RED. Constants must be referenced with a dotted name (Color.RED, module.RED) so Python treats them as value patterns compared with ==.

How do positional class patterns like Point(0, y) work?

Python checks isinstance(subject, Point), then maps positional sub-patterns to attribute names using Point.__match_args__ — here ('x', 'y') — and matches subject.x against 0 and binds subject.y to y. Dataclasses and named tuples define __match_args__ automatically.

Strings & Numbers

Strings

str is an immutable sequence of Unicode characters — indexing, slicing and methods all return new strings.

Indexing & slicing

s[start:stop:step] — start is included, stop is excluded, negative indices count from the end.

Python
s = "interview"
print(s[0], s[-1], len(s))      # i w 9
print(s[0:5], s[5:], s[-4:])    # inter view view
print(s[::-1], s[::2])          # weivretni itriw
Immutability
Python
s = "cat"
try:
    s[0] = "b"
except TypeError as e:
    print("TypeError:", e)       # TypeError: 'str' object does not support item assignment
s = "b" + s[1:]                  # build a new string instead
print(s)                         # bat
Performance

Building a string with += in a loop can be O(n²). Collect pieces in a list and call ''.join(parts) once — O(n).

Essential methods
MethodDoesExample → result
split(sep) / join(it)String ↔ list'a,b'.split(',') → ['a', 'b']
strip() / lstrip() / rstrip()Trim whitespace (or given chars)' hi '.strip() → 'hi'
lower() / upper() / title()Change case'Py'.lower() → 'py'
find(x) / index(x)Position or −1 / raises'abc'.find('z') → -1
replace(a, b)Replace all occurrences'aa'.replace('a','b') → 'bb'
startswith / endswithPrefix / suffix test'file.py'.endswith('.py') → True
isdigit / isalpha / isalnumCharacter classes'123'.isdigit() → True
count(x)Non-overlapping occurrences'banana'.count('a') → 3
Python
words = "  the quick  brown fox ".split()   # split() with no args handles runs of spaces
print(words)                                 # ['the', 'quick', 'brown', 'fox']
print("-".join(words))                       # the-quick-brown-fox
print(" ".join(w.capitalize() for w in words))   # The Quick Brown Fox
Go deeper

This page is the overview. The next topics go deeper: String Methods Deep Dive, String Formatting & f-strings, Unicode, Bytes & Encodings and Regular Expressions.

f-strings & formatting
Python
name, score, ratio = "Asha", 93, 0.8567
print(f"{name} scored {score}")          # Asha scored 93
print(f"{ratio:.2%} | {score:05d}")      # 85.67% | 00093
print(f"{name!r:>8}|{score:<5}|")        #   'Asha'|93   |
print(f"{score=}")                       # score=93
print(f"{1_000_000:,}")                  # 1,000,000
Characters, Unicode & bytes
Python
print(ord("A"), chr(97))                 # 65 a
print(len("naïve"), len("naïve".encode("utf-8")))   # 5 6
print("é".encode(), b"\xc3\xa9".decode())          # b'\xc3\xa9' é

str

Text — a sequence of Unicode code points.

bytes

Raw 8-bit data — files, network, hashing. Convert with encode() / decode().

Interview favourites
Python
def is_palindrome(s: str) -> bool:
    cleaned = [c.lower() for c in s if c.isalnum()]
    return cleaned == cleaned[::-1]

def is_anagram(a: str, b: str) -> bool:
    from collections import Counter
    return Counter(a) == Counter(b)

print(is_palindrome("A man, a plan, a canal: Panama"))   # True
print(is_anagram("listen", "silent"))                    # True
Strings & Numbers

String Methods Deep Dive

str has ~47 methods. Knowing the right one — partition instead of a regex, casefold instead of lower, removeprefix instead of strip — makes text code shorter and correct.

Remember

Every method returns a new string (or a list / bool / int). Nothing modifies the original — strings are immutable.

Case conversion
MethodDoesExample → result
lower() / upper()All lower / upper case'PyThon'.lower() → 'python'
casefold()Aggressive lower-casing for caseless comparison'Straße'.casefold() → 'strasse'
title()First letter of every word upper'hello world'.title() → 'Hello World'
capitalize()First character upper, rest lower'hELLO'.capitalize() → 'Hello'
swapcase()Invert each letter's case'Ab'.swapcase() → 'aB'
Python
print("Straße".lower(), "Straße".casefold())          # straße strasse
print("STRASSE".casefold() == "Straße".casefold())    # True
print("STRASSE".lower() == "Straße".lower())          # False
print("they're bill's".title())                      # They'Re Bill'S
print(" ".join(w.capitalize() for w in "they're bill's".split()))   # They're Bill's
Watch out

title() treats every non-letter as a word boundary, so apostrophes break it (They'Re). Use capitalize() per word, or string.capwords(s).

Searching
MethodReturnsIf not found
s.find(sub[, start[, end]])Lowest index-1
s.rfind(sub)Highest index-1
s.index(sub) / s.rindex(sub)Like find / rfindraises ValueError
s.count(sub)Number of non-overlapping occurrences0
s.startswith(p) / s.endswith(p)bool — p may be a tuple of optionsFalse
sub in sbool — the most readable testFalse
Python
path = "reports/2026/q1.final.csv"
print(path.find("/"), path.rfind("/"), path.find("zip"))   # 7 12 -1
print(path[path.rfind(".") + 1:])                         # csv
print(path.endswith((".csv", ".tsv")))                    # True
print("aaaa".count("aa"), "2026" in path)                 # 2 True
print("banana".find("a", 2), "banana".find("a", 2, 3))    # 3 -1
Key point

Use find when "not found" is a normal outcome (check for -1); use index when absence is a bug and an exception is what you want. For a plain yes/no, in beats both.

Splitting family
MethodBehaviour
s.split()Split on runs of any whitespace, dropping empty strings and leading/trailing space
s.split(sep)Split on an exact separator — keeps empty strings between adjacent separators
s.split(sep, maxsplit)At most maxsplit splits, from the left
s.rsplit(sep, maxsplit)At most maxsplit splits, from the right
s.splitlines()Split on \n, \r\n, \r… without a trailing empty item
s.partition(sep)Always a 3-tuple (before, sep, after) — at the first sep
s.rpartition(sep)Same, at the last sep
Python
print("  a   b  c ".split())               # ['a', 'b', 'c']
print("a,,b,".split(","))                  # ['a', '', 'b', '']
print("k=v=w".split("=", 1))               # ['k', 'v=w']
print("a.b.c.txt".rsplit(".", 1))          # ['a.b.c', 'txt']
print("one\ntwo\r\nthree\n".splitlines())  # ['one', 'two', 'three']
print("one\ntwo\n".split("\n"))            # ['one', 'two', '']
print("user@example.com".partition("@"))   # ('user', '@', 'example.com')
print("no-at-sign".partition("@"))         # ('no-at-sign', '', '')
key, _, value = "timeout=30".partition("=")
print(key, value)                          # timeout 30
Key point

partition never raises and always returns three parts — perfect for key, _, value = line.partition('=') parsing without an if.

Trimming & prefixes
Python
print("  hi  ".strip() + "|")               # hi|
print("xxhixyx".strip("xy"))                # hi   ← a SET of characters, not a substring
print("www.python.org".lstrip("w."))        # python.org
print("test_login.py".removesuffix(".py"))  # test_login
print("mississippi".rstrip("ip"))           # mississ
print("mississippi".removesuffix("ippi"))   # mississ
print("reader.txt".rstrip(".txt"))          # reader   ← looks right…
print("rat.txt".rstrip(".txt"))             # ra       ← …but eats the 't' of 'rat'
print("rat.txt".removesuffix(".txt"))       # rat
Classic bug

strip/lstrip/rstrip(chars) remove any combination of those characters, not a prefix/suffix string. To remove an exact prefix or suffix use removeprefix / removesuffix (3.9+).

Padding & alignment
Python
print("[" + "hi".center(8, "*") + "]")      # [***hi***]
print("[" + "hi".ljust(6) + "]")            # [hi    ]
print("[" + "hi".rjust(6, ".") + "]")       # [....hi]
print("42".zfill(5), "-42".zfill(5))        # 00042 -0042
print("a\tbc\td".expandtabs(4))             # a   bc  d
print("-" * 10)                             # ----------
Key point

For tables and reports, f-string alignment (f'{x:>8}') does the same job inline — see String Formatting.

Replacing & translating
Python
s = "a-b-c-d"
print(s.replace("-", "+"))                  # a+b+c+d
print(s.replace("-", "", 2))                # abc-d   ← only the first 2
table = str.maketrans({"a": "4", "e": "3", "o": "0"})
print("hello world".translate(table))       # h3ll0 w0rld
strip_vowels = str.maketrans("", "", "aeiou")   # 3rd arg: characters to delete
print("programming".translate(strip_vowels))    # prgrmmng
print("abc".translate(str.maketrans("abc", "xyz")))   # xyz

translate does many single-character replacements (and deletions) in one pass — much faster than chaining replace calls.

Character-class predicates
MethodTrue when every character is…'²''½''٣' (Arabic 3)
isdecimal()a decimal digit usable by int()FalseFalseTrue
isdigit()a digit, including superscriptsTrueFalseTrue
isnumeric()any numeric character, incl. fractionsTrueTrueTrue
Python
for s in ["123", "²", "½", "٣", "-5", "3.5", ""]:
    print(repr(s), s.isdecimal(), s.isdigit(), s.isnumeric())
Output
'123' True True True
'²' False True True
'½' False False True
'٣' True True True
'-5' False False False
'3.5' False False False
'' False False False
Python
print("abc123".isalnum(), "abc".isalpha(), "ABC".isupper())    # True True True
print(" \t\n".isspace(), "".isspace())                          # True False
print("my_var".isidentifier(), "2fast".isidentifier())          # True False
print(int("٣") + 1)                                             # 4
Watch out

None of the digit predicates accept '-5' or '3.5'. To check "is this a number?", just try int(s) / float(s) and catch ValueError (EAFP). Also note all predicates return False for the empty string.

Comparison, repetition & membership
Python
print("apple" < "banana", "Zebra" < "apple")     # True True   ← 'Z' (90) < 'a' (97)
print(sorted(["b", "C", "a"]))                   # ['C', 'a', 'b']
print(sorted(["b", "C", "a"], key=str.casefold)) # ['a', 'b', 'C']
print("10" < "9", int("10") < int("9"))          # True False
print("ab" * 3, "-" * 0 == "")                   # ababab True
print("ell" in "hello", "" in "hello")           # True True
Watch out

String comparison is lexicographic by code point: uppercase sorts before lowercase and '10' < '9'. Sort with key=str.casefold for human order and convert numeric strings before comparing.

Building strings efficiently
Do

Collect pieces in a list and call ''.join(parts) once — O(total length). join needs strings: ', '.join(map(str, nums)).

Avoid

s += piece inside a big loop — each += may copy the whole string (O(n²) worst case). CPython sometimes optimises it, but don't rely on it.

Python
nums = [3, 1, 4]
print(", ".join(map(str, nums)))      # 3, 1, 4
print("".join(reversed("stressed")))  # desserts
try:
    ", ".join(nums)
except TypeError as e:
    print(type(e).__name__)           # TypeError
Interview questions
What's the difference between find and index?

Both return the lowest index of a substring. find returns -1 when it's missing; index raises ValueError. Lists only have index.

Why does 'rat.txt'.rstrip('.txt') return 'ra'?

rstrip takes a set of characters and strips any of them from the end — t, x, . — until it hits a character not in the set. Use removesuffix('.txt') to remove an exact suffix.

lower() vs casefold()?

casefold() is a stronger normalisation meant for caseless matching: it also maps characters like ß → ss. Use casefold() when comparing user text case-insensitively; lower() for display.

How do you reverse the words of a sentence?
Python
s = "  the sky  is blue "
print(" ".join(reversed(s.split())))   # blue is sky the

split() with no arguments discards extra whitespace, so the result has single spaces.

Strings & Numbers

String Formatting & f-strings

f-strings are the modern way to build text: any expression inside {}, plus a compact format spec after : that controls width, alignment, precision, signs and number bases.

f-string basics
Python
name, items, price = "Asha", 3, 49.5
print(f"{name} bought {items} items")            # Asha bought 3 items
print(f"total = {items * price}")                # total = 148.5
print(f"{name.upper()} {len(name)} {'yes' if items > 2 else 'no'}")   # ASHA 4 yes
print(f"{{literal braces}} {items}")             # {literal braces} 3
data = {"k": [10, 20]}
print(f"{data['k'][1]}")                         # 20
PartSyntaxExample
Expression{expr}f'{a + b}'
Conversion{expr!r} / !s / !arepr(), str(), ascii() of the value
Format spec{expr:spec}f'{pi:.3f}'
Self-documenting{expr=}f'{x=}' → x=5 (3.8+)
Nested spec{expr:{width}.{prec}f}Width/precision from variables
Literal brace{{ and }}f'{{x}}' → {x}
Debugging with = and conversions
Python
x, word = 42, "café"
print(f"{x=}")                  # x=42
print(f"{x * 2 = }")            # x * 2 = 84
print(f"{word=}")               # word='café'   ← = uses repr() by default
print(f"{word!s}", f"{word!r}", f"{word!a}")   # café 'café' 'caf\xe9'
print(f"{x = :>6}|")            # x =     42|
Tip

{x=} prints the expression text, then the value's repr() — the quickest print-debugging tool in Python.

The format-spec mini-language
[[fill]align][sign][z][#][0][width][grouping][.precision][type]
FieldOptionsMeaning
fillany characterPad character (needs an align after it)
align< > ^ =Left / right / centre / pad after the sign
sign+ - spaceAlways show sign / only negatives (default) / space for positives
#Alternate form: 0b, 0o, 0x prefixes
0Zero-pad numbers (sign-aware)
widthintegerMinimum field width
grouping, or _Thousands separator
.precisionintegerDigits after the point (f, %), significant digits (g), or max chars for strings
typed b o x X e f g % n s cPresentation: decimal, binary, octal, hex, exponent, fixed, general, percent…
Alignment & padding
Python
s = "py"
print(f"[{s:<6}] [{s:>6}] [{s:^6}]")      # [py    ] [    py] [  py  ]
print(f"[{s:*^8}] [{s:->5}]")             # [***py***] [---py]
print(f"[{42:5}] [{'ab':5}]")             # [   42] [ab   ]   ← numbers right, strings left by default
print(f"[{-42:=+7}] [{-42:07}]")          # [-    42] [-000042]
print(f"[{'truncate me':.5}]")            # [trunc]
Numbers: precision, signs, grouping
Python
pi, big, ratio = 3.14159265, 1234567.891, 0.4567
print(f"{pi:.2f} {pi:.4f} {pi:8.3f}|")    # 3.14 3.1416    3.142|
print(f"{big:,.2f} {big:_.1f}")           # 1,234,567.89 1_234_567.9
print(f"{ratio:.1%} {ratio:%}")           # 45.7% 45.670000%
print(f"{big:e} {big:.2e}")               # 1.234568e+06 1.23e+06
print(f"{pi:g} {big:g} {0.00001234:g}")   # 3.14159 1.23457e+06 1.234e-05
print(f"{5:+d} {-5:+d} {5: d}")           # +5 -5  5
print(f"{1_000_000:,}")                   # 1,000,000
Integers in other bases
Python
n = 255
print(f"{n:b} {n:o} {n:x} {n:X}")         # 11111111 377 ff FF
print(f"{n:#b} {n:#o} {n:#x}")            # 0b11111111 0o377 0xff
print(f"{5:08b}")                         # 00000101
print(f"{n:#06x}")                        # 0x00ff   ← width counts the prefix
print(f"{65:c}")                          # A
print(format(n, "x"), int("ff", 16))      # ff 255
Nested & dynamic specs
Python
rows = [("apple", 1.5), ("kiwi", 12.25)]
w = max(len(name) for name, _ in rows)
for name, cost in rows:
    print(f"{name:<{w}} | {cost:>{8}.{2}f}")
Output
apple |     1.50
kiwi  |    12.25
Dates in f-strings
Python
from datetime import datetime
d = datetime(2026, 9, 29, 14, 5)
print(f"{d:%Y-%m-%d %H:%M}")        # 2026-09-29 14:05
print(f"{d:%d %b %Y, %A}")          # 29 Sep 2026, Tuesday
print(f"{d:%I:%M %p}")              # 02:05 PM
Key point

A format spec is passed to the object's __format__ method — that is why datetime understands strftime codes after the colon.

__format__ for your own classes
Python
class Money:
    def __init__(self, amount):
        self.amount = amount
    def __format__(self, spec):
        if spec == "inr":
            return f"₹{self.amount:,.2f}"
        return format(self.amount, spec)     # fall back to float formatting

m = Money(1234.5)
print(f"{m:inr}")              # ₹1,234.50
print(f"{m:>10.1f}|")          #     1234.5|
print(format(m, "inr"))        # ₹1,234.50
Older styles: str.format, % and Template
Python
print("{} + {} = {}".format(1, 2, 3))              # 1 + 2 = 3
print("{0}{1}{0}".format("ab", "-"))               # ab-ab
print("{name} is {age}".format(name="Ravi", age=30))   # Ravi is 30
point = {"x": 3, "y": 4}
print("({p[x]}, {p[y]})".format(p=point))          # (3, 4)
print("{:>6.2f}|".format(3.14159))                 #   3.14|

print("%s has %d items (%.1f%%)" % ("cart", 3, 42.456))   # cart has 3 items (42.5%)
print("%(who)s says hi" % {"who": "Asha"})         # Asha says hi

from string import Template
t = Template("Hello $name, you owe $$${amount}")
print(t.substitute(name="Kiran", amount=20))       # Hello Kiran, you owe $20
print(t.safe_substitute(name="Kiran"))             # Hello Kiran, you owe $${amount}
StyleUse it for
f-stringsDefault choice — fastest and most readable (3.6+)
str.formatTemplates stored separately from the values, e.g. FMT.format(**row)
% formattingLegacy code and logging calls (log.info('%s', x) formats lazily)
string.TemplateTemplates written by users — $name only, no expressions, so it's safe
Security

Never call .format() or build f-strings from untrusted template text — '{0.__class__}'.format(obj) can read attributes. Use string.Template for user-supplied templates.

Python 3.12+: f-strings grew up
Python
songs = ["a", "b"]
print(f"{", ".join(songs)}")          # a, b     ← same quotes inside (3.12+)
print(f"{'\n'.join(songs)!r}")        # 'a\nb'   ← backslashes allowed in expressions
total = f"{
    sum([1, 2, 3])                   # comments and line breaks inside {} are fine
}"
print(total)                          # 6
Version

PEP 701 (3.12) made f-strings part of the real grammar: reuse the outer quote type, write backslashes and comments inside the braces, and nest arbitrarily. Python 3.14 adds t-strings (t'…') that produce a Template object for safe, deferred processing (e.g. SQL/HTML escaping).

Pitfalls
  • f'{x:.2f}' on a string raises ValueError: Unknown format code 'f' — convert first.
  • round(x, 2) changes the value; f'{x:.2f}' only changes how it is displayed. For money use Decimal.
  • = in {x=} uses repr, so strings appear quoted — add !s for the plain form.
  • Forgetting the f prefix silently prints the braces: '{name}'.
How do you print a float with 2 decimals and thousands separators, right-aligned in 12 characters?
Python
print(f"{1234567.891:>12,.2f}|")   # 1,234,567.89|

Spec parts in order: align >, width 12, grouping ,, precision .2, type f.

Which string formatting method is fastest?

f-strings — they compile to direct bytecode (FORMAT_VALUE / BUILD_STRING) with no method lookup or argument parsing, so they beat str.format and %.

Strings & Numbers

Unicode, Bytes & Encodings

A str is a sequence of Unicode code points; bytes is a sequence of raw 8-bit values. An encoding such as UTF-8 is the bridge — and most text bugs come from crossing that bridge carelessly.

Code point — a number that identifies one Unicode character, written U+0041 for A. ord() gives it, chr() goes back. Python strings index and measure in code points.

str (text)
→
.encode('utf-8')
→
bytes (disk / network)
→
.decode('utf-8')
→
str
Code points & escapes
Python
print(ord("A"), hex(ord("€")), chr(0x1F600))       # 65 0x20ac 😀
print("\u00e9", "\U0001F40D", "\N{GREEK SMALL LETTER PI}")   # é 🐍 π
import unicodedata
print(unicodedata.name("€"))                       # EURO SIGN
print(unicodedata.category("A"), unicodedata.category("5"))   # Lu Nd
EscapeMeaningExample
\xhhCode point with 2 hex digits'\xe9' → é
\uhhhh4 hex digits (BMP)'\u20ac' → €
\Uhhhhhhhh8 hex digits (any code point)'\U0001F600' → 😀
\N{name}By official Unicode name'\N{SNOWMAN}' → ☃
UTF-8: variable-length encoding
Python
for ch in ["A", "é", "€", "😀"]:
    b = ch.encode("utf-8")
    print(ch, len(b), b)
Output
A 1 b'A'
é 2 b'\xc3\xa9'
€ 3 b'\xe2\x82\xac'
😀 4 b'\xf0\x9f\x98\x80'
Python
s = "naïve café"
print(len(s), len(s.encode("utf-8")), len(s.encode("utf-16")))   # 10 12 22
print(s.encode("utf-8")[:3])                                    # b'na\xc3'

UTF-8

1–4 bytes per code point, ASCII-compatible, no byte-order issues. The default everywhere — web, Linux, Python source files.

UTF-16 / UTF-32

2–4 / always 4 bytes. Used internally by Windows, Java and JavaScript. Need a byte-order mark (BOM).

Latin-1 (ISO-8859-1)

One byte per character, only 256 characters. Decoding never fails — every byte maps to something (often wrong).

ASCII

7-bit, 128 characters. Any non-English text raises UnicodeEncodeError.

Encoding errors and errors=
Python
data = "café".encode("utf-8")
print(data.decode("utf-8"))                        # café
print(data.decode("latin-1"))                      # cafÃ©   ← mojibake: wrong codec
try:
    data.decode("ascii")
except UnicodeDecodeError as e:
    print(e.reason, e.start)                       # ordinal not in range(128) 3
print(data.decode("ascii", errors="replace"))      # caf��
print(data.decode("ascii", errors="ignore"))       # caf
print("café".encode("ascii", errors="xmlcharrefreplace"))   # b'caf&#233;'
print("café".encode("ascii", errors="backslashreplace"))    # b'caf\\xe9'
errors=On bad data
'strict' (default)Raise UnicodeEncodeError / UnicodeDecodeError
'replace'Substitute ? (encode) or U+FFFD � (decode)
'ignore'Silently drop — loses data
'backslashreplace'Write escapes like \xe9
'surrogateescape'Round-trip undecodable bytes (used for OS file names)
Watch out

Mojibake (cafÃ©) means text was encoded with one codec and decoded with another. Fix it at the boundary — always state the encoding when you read or write.

bytes, bytearray & memoryview
Python
b = b"hi\x00\xff"
print(b[0], b[-1], list(b))            # 104 255 [104, 105, 0, 255]
print(b[:2], type(b[:2]).__name__)     # b'hi' bytes
print(b.hex(), bytes.fromhex("48 69"))  # 686900ff b'Hi'
print(bytes([72, 105]), bytes(3))      # b'Hi' b'\x00\x00\x00'

buf = bytearray(b"hello")              # mutable
buf[0] = ord("J")
buf.extend(b"!")
print(buf, bytes(buf))                 # bytearray(b'Jello!') b'Jello!'

view = memoryview(buf)[1:4]            # zero-copy slice
view[0] = ord("E")
print(buf)                             # bytearray(b'JEllo!')
TypeMutableIndexing givesUse for
strNoa 1-char strHuman text
bytesNoan int 0–255Binary data, network payloads, hashes
bytearrayYesan int 0–255Building/patching binary buffers
memoryviewFollows the bufferan intZero-copy slices of large buffers
Watch out

You can't mix them: 'a' + b'b' raises TypeError. Decode bytes to text as early as possible and encode as late as possible (the Unicode sandwich).

Normalization: same text, different code points
Python
import unicodedata
a = "café"                        # é as one code point U+00E9
b = "cafe\u0301"                  # e + COMBINING ACUTE ACCENT
print(a == b)                     # False   ← both render as "café"
print(len(a), len(b))             # 4 5
nfc = lambda s: unicodedata.normalize("NFC", s)
print(nfc(a) == nfc(b))           # True
print(len(unicodedata.normalize("NFD", a)))   # 5
print(unicodedata.normalize("NFKC", "ﬁ①"))   # fi1
FormDoesUse
NFCCompose into single code points where possibleStoring and comparing text (the web default)
NFDDecompose into base + combining marksStripping accents
NFKC / NFKDAlso fold compatibility forms: ﬁ → fi, ① → 1Search, identifiers, user names
Python
import unicodedata
def strip_accents(s):
    decomposed = unicodedata.normalize("NFD", s)
    return "".join(c for c in decomposed if not unicodedata.combining(c))

print(strip_accents("Crème brûlée à São Paulo"))   # Creme brulee a Sao Paulo
def same_text(x, y):
    norm = lambda s: unicodedata.normalize("NFC", s).casefold()
    return norm(x) == norm(y)
print(same_text("STRASSE", "straße"))             # True
Length isn't what users see
Python
thumbs = "👍🏽"                        # thumbs up + skin-tone modifier
family = "👨\u200d👩\u200d👧"           # joined with ZERO WIDTH JOINERs
flag = "🇮🇳"                           # two regional-indicator letters
print(len(thumbs), len(family), len(flag))   # 2 5 2
print(thumbs[::-1] == thumbs)              # False   ← reversing splits the emoji
Key point

A user-perceived character (a grapheme cluster) can be several code points. len, slicing and [::-1] work on code points, so they can cut emoji or accented letters in half. For true grapheme handling use the third-party grapheme or regex (\X) modules.

Files & encodings
Python
import tempfile, os
path = os.path.join(tempfile.mkdtemp(), "bom.csv")
with open(path, "w", encoding="utf-8-sig") as f:     # writes a BOM (Excel likes it)
    f.write("name\nAsha\n")
with open(path, "rb") as f:
    print(f.read(3))                                  # b'\xef\xbb\xbf'
with open(path, encoding="utf-8") as f:
    print(repr(f.readline()))                         # '\ufeffname\n'
with open(path, encoding="utf-8-sig") as f:           # strips the BOM
    print(repr(f.readline()))                         # 'name\n'
  • Always pass encoding='utf-8' to open() in text mode — the default depends on the OS locale (Python 3.15 makes UTF-8 the default).
  • Open binary files ('rb', 'wb') for images, zips and anything you'll hash — you get bytes, no decoding.
  • utf-8-sig reads files with or without a BOM; use it for CSVs from Excel.
  • sys.getdefaultencoding() is always 'utf-8'; locale.getpreferredencoding() is what open() uses by default.
Interview questions
What is the difference between str and bytes?

str is text: a sequence of Unicode code points. bytes is binary data: a sequence of integers 0–255. You convert with str.encode(encoding) and bytes.decode(encoding); you can't concatenate them directly.

Why is len('é') sometimes 1 and sometimes 2?

é can be one precomposed code point (U+00E9) or e plus a combining accent (U+0301). Both render the same. Normalize with unicodedata.normalize('NFC', s) before comparing or measuring.

What is UTF-8 and why is it the default?

A variable-length encoding of Unicode using 1–4 bytes per code point. It is backward-compatible with ASCII, has no byte-order issues, is compact for English text, and can encode every character — so it became the standard for files, the web and Python source code.

Strings & Numbers

Numbers: int, float, Decimal & Fraction

Python has unlimited-size integers, IEEE-754 binary floats, exact Decimal and Fraction types and a rich math module. Picking the right one avoids rounding bugs and overflow.

TypePrecisionSpeedUse for
intExact, unlimited sizeFastCounting, indices, IDs, big-number math
float~15–17 significant digits (64-bit binary)Fastest (hardware)Science, measurements, ML — where tiny errors are fine
decimal.DecimalExact decimal, configurable precisionSlowerMoney, financial rounding rules
fractions.FractionExact rational p/qSlowestExact ratios, probability, teaching
complexPair of floatsFastSignal processing, maths (2 + 3j)
Integers: unlimited & many literals
Python
print(2 ** 100)                               # 1267650600228229401496703205376
print(0b1010, 0o17, 0xFF, 1_000_000)          # 10 15 255 1000000
print(int("1010", 2), int("z", 36), int(" 42 "))   # 10 35 42
n = 37
print(n.bit_length(), n.bit_count(), bin(n))  # 6 3 0b100101
print(7 // 2, -7 // 2, -7 % 3, int(-3.9))     # 3 -4 2 -3
print(divmod(17, 5), divmod(-17, 5))          # (3, 2) (-4, 3)
Key point

// and % round toward negative infinity, so -7 // 2 == -4 and -7 % 3 == 2 (the result has the sign of the divisor). C and Java truncate toward zero. int() truncates toward zero.

Python
print(pow(2, 10), pow(2, 10, 1000))    # 1024 24     ← fast modular exponentiation
print(pow(3, -1, 7))                   # 5           ← modular inverse: 3 * 5 % 7 == 1
print(True + True, isinstance(True, int))   # 2 True
print((255).to_bytes(2, "big"), int.from_bytes(b"\x01\x00", "big"))   # b'\x00\xff' 256
Watch out

Converting a huge int to/from str is limited to 4300 digits by default (a DoS protection since 3.11): str(10**5000) raises ValueError. Raise it with sys.set_int_max_str_digits(n) if you really need to.

Floats are binary approximations
Python
print(0.1 + 0.2)                        # 0.30000000000000004
print(0.1 + 0.2 == 0.3)                 # False
import math
print(math.isclose(0.1 + 0.2, 0.3))     # True
print((0.1).as_integer_ratio())         # (3602879701896397, 36028797018963968)
print(f"{0.1:.20f}")                    # 0.10000000000000000555
total = 0.0
for _ in range(10):
    total += 0.1                        # error accumulates step by step
print(total, math.fsum([0.1] * 10))     # 0.9999999999999999 1.0
print(sum([0.1] * 10))                  # 1.0   ← sum() of floats is compensated since 3.12
print(2 ** 53 + 1.0 == 2 ** 53)         # True   ← integers above 2**53 lose precision

IEEE 754 double — 64 bits: 1 sign bit, 11 exponent bits, 52 fraction bits. Only numbers of the form m × 2ᵉ are exact, so decimal fractions like 0.1 are stored as the nearest binary value.

Do

Compare floats with math.isclose(a, b, rel_tol=1e-9, abs_tol=…); sum many floats with math.fsum; keep money in integer cents or Decimal.

Avoid

if total == 0.3: or storing prices as floats — errors accumulate and comparisons fail.

inf, nan & float limits
Python
import math, sys
inf, nan = float("inf"), float("nan")
print(inf > 10**308, -inf < 0, inf - inf)     # True True nan
print(nan == nan, math.isnan(nan))            # False True
print(1e308 * 10, 2.0 ** -1075)               # inf 0.0
print(sys.float_info.max, sys.float_info.epsilon)   # 1.7976931348623157e+308 2.220446049250313e-16
print(sorted([3, nan, 1]))                    # [3, nan, 1]   ← nan breaks sorting
Watch out

nan is not equal to anything, including itself, so x in list, sort, max and dict lookups behave strangely with it. Filter with math.isnan first. Also 1 / 0 raises ZeroDivisionError — Python does not return inf for division.

Rounding
Python
import math
print(round(2.5), round(3.5), round(-2.5))       # 2 4 -2   ← banker's rounding
print(round(2.675, 2))                           # 2.67     ← 2.675 is really 2.67499999…
print(round(1234.5678, -2))                      # 1200.0
print(math.floor(-2.5), math.ceil(-2.5), math.trunc(-2.5))   # -3 -2 -2
print(int(2.99), math.floor(2.99))               # 2 2
FunctionRounds2.5-2.5
round(x)To nearest, ties to even2-2
math.floor(x)Down (toward −∞)2-3
math.ceil(x)Up (toward +∞)3-2
math.trunc(x) / int(x)Toward zero2-2
Decimal.quantize(…, ROUND_HALF_UP)Ties away from zero (school rounding)3-3
Decimal: exact decimal arithmetic
Python
from decimal import Decimal, ROUND_HALF_UP, getcontext, localcontext
print(Decimal("0.1") + Decimal("0.2"))               # 0.3
print(Decimal(0.1))                                  # 0.1000000000000000055511151231257827021181583404541015625
price = Decimal("19.99")
total = price * 3
print(total, total * Decimal("1.18"))                # 59.97 70.7646
tax = (total * Decimal("0.18")).quantize(Decimal("0.01"), rounding=ROUND_HALF_UP)
print(tax)                                           # 10.79
print(Decimal("2.5").quantize(Decimal("1"), rounding=ROUND_HALF_UP))   # 3
print(getcontext().prec)                             # 28
with localcontext() as ctx:
    ctx.prec = 5
    print(Decimal(1) / Decimal(7))                   # 0.14286
Watch out

Build Decimal from strings (Decimal('0.1')), never from floats — Decimal(0.1) faithfully copies the float's binary error. Also don't mix Decimal and float in arithmetic: it raises TypeError.

Fraction & complex
Python
from fractions import Fraction
f = Fraction(1, 3) + Fraction(1, 6)
print(f, f.numerator, f.denominator)            # 1/2 1 2
print(Fraction("0.75"), Fraction(0.1).limit_denominator(100))   # 3/4 1/10
print(float(Fraction(22, 7)))                   # 3.142857142857143

z = 3 + 4j
print(z.real, z.imag, abs(z), z.conjugate())    # 3.0 4.0 5.0 (3-4j)
import cmath
print(cmath.sqrt(-1))                           # 1j
The math module
FunctionDoesExample
sqrt, isqrtFloat root / exact integer floor rootmath.isqrt(17) → 4
gcd, lcmGreatest common divisor / least common multiple (any number of args)math.lcm(4, 6) → 12
comb, perm, factorialnCr, nPr, n!math.comb(5, 2) → 10
prod, fsumProduct / precise float summath.prod([2, 3, 4]) → 24
log, log2, log10, expLogarithms (log(x, base))math.log2(1024) → 10.0
hypot, distEuclidean length / distance between pointsmath.dist((0, 0), (3, 4)) → 5.0
floor, ceil, truncRounding to intmath.ceil(2.1) → 3
pi, e, tau, inf, nanConstantsmath.tau == 2 * math.pi
isclose, isfinite, isnanFloat checksmath.isclose(0.1 + 0.2, 0.3)
Python
import math
print(math.isqrt(10**20), math.sqrt(2))        # 10000000000 1.4142135623730951
print(math.gcd(12, 18, 30), math.lcm(4, 6))    # 6 12
print(math.comb(52, 5), math.perm(5, 2), math.factorial(10))   # 2598960 20 3628800
print(math.prod(range(1, 6)), math.log2(1024), math.log(8, 2))  # 120 10.0 3.0
print(math.hypot(3, 4), math.dist((1, 1), (4, 5)))   # 5.0 5.0
print(math.degrees(math.pi), round(math.sin(math.pi / 6), 3))   # 180.0 0.5
Key point

Use math.isqrt(n) — not int(math.sqrt(n)) — for perfect-square checks on big integers: the float version is wrong once n exceeds 2⁵³.

Interview questions
Why is 0.1 + 0.2 != 0.3?

Floats are binary (IEEE 754). 0.1, 0.2 and 0.3 have no exact binary representation, so each is stored as the nearest double; the rounding errors don't cancel. Compare with math.isclose, or use Decimal / integer cents when exactness matters.

Can Python integers overflow?

No. int is arbitrary precision — it grows to use as much memory as needed (slower as it grows). Floats can overflow to inf, and numpy fixed-width integers do wrap around.

What does round(2.5) return and why?

2. Python 3 uses round-half-to-even (banker's rounding), which avoids a systematic upward bias when rounding many values. Use Decimal.quantize(..., rounding=ROUND_HALF_UP) for school-style rounding.

How would you check whether a large number is a perfect square?
Python
import math
def is_square(n):
    return n >= 0 and math.isqrt(n) ** 2 == n
print(is_square(10**30), is_square(10**30 + 1))   # True False
Strings & Numbers

Regular Expressions (re)

A regular expression is a tiny pattern language for matching text. Python's re module finds, extracts, validates, splits and rewrites strings with it.

Rule #1

Always write patterns as raw strings — r'\d+' — so Python doesn't interpret the backslashes before re sees them.

The five ways to match
FunctionReturnsMatches where?
re.match(p, s)Match or NoneOnly at the start of s
re.search(p, s)Match or NoneThe first place anywhere in s
re.fullmatch(p, s)Match or NoneThe whole string — best for validation
re.findall(p, s)list of strings (or tuples of groups)Every non-overlapping match
re.finditer(p, s)Iterator of Match objectsEvery match, lazily, with positions
Python
import re
s = "Order 66 shipped on 2026-09-29"
print(re.match(r"\d+", s))                    # None   ← s doesn't start with a digit
m = re.search(r"\d+", s)
print(m.group(), m.start(), m.end(), m.span())   # 66 6 8 (6, 8)
print(re.findall(r"\d+", s))                  # ['66', '2026', '09', '29']
print(bool(re.fullmatch(r"\d{4}", "2026")), bool(re.fullmatch(r"\d{4}", "20261")))   # True False
print([m.start() for m in re.finditer(r"\d+", s)])   # [6, 20, 25, 28]
Watch out

re.match only anchors at the start, not the end: re.match(r'\d+', '12abc') succeeds. For validation use re.fullmatch (or ^…$ with search).

Pattern syntax cheat sheet
PatternMatches
.Any character except newline (any at all with re.DOTALL)
\d / \DDigit / non-digit (Unicode digits by default)
\w / \WWord char [A-Za-z0-9_] + Unicode letters / anything else
\s / \SWhitespace / non-whitespace
[abc], [a-z], [^0-9]Character class, range, negated class
^ / $Start / end of string (of each line with re.MULTILINE)
\A / \ZStart / end of string, always
\b / \BWord boundary / not a boundary
a|bAlternation: a or b
\., \*, \(Escaped literal metacharacters (or use re.escape(text))
QuantifierMeansLazy version
*0 or more*?
+1 or more+?
?0 or 1 (optional)??
{n}Exactly n—
{n,m}Between n and m{n,m}?
{n,}n or more{n,}?
Python
import re
text = "cat catalog concat Cat"
print(re.findall(r"cat", text))              # ['cat', 'cat', 'cat']
print(re.findall(r"\bcat\b", text))          # ['cat']
print(re.findall(r"\bcat\b", text, re.I))    # ['cat', 'Cat']
print(re.findall(r"colou?r", "color colour"))    # ['color', 'colour']
print(re.findall(r"[A-Z][a-z]+", "Asha met Ravi in Pune"))   # ['Asha', 'Ravi', 'Pune']
print(re.findall(r"\d{2,3}", "1 12 123 1234"))  # ['12', '123', '123']
print(re.escape("1+1=2?"))                   # 1\+1=2\?
Groups: capture, name, skip
Python
import re
m = re.search(r"(\d{4})-(\d{2})-(\d{2})", "due 2026-09-29!")
print(m.group(0), m.group(1), m.groups())    # 2026-09-29 2026 ('2026', '09', '29')

m = re.search(r"(?P<user>[\w.]+)@(?P<domain>[\w.]+)", "mail asha.k@lvn.io now")
print(m["user"], m.group("domain"))          # asha.k lvn.io
print(m.groupdict())                         # {'user': 'asha.k', 'domain': 'lvn.io'}

print(re.findall(r"(\w+)=(\d+)", "a=1, b=22"))       # [('a', '1'), ('b', '22')]
print(re.findall(r"(?:ab)+", "ababab xab"))          # ['ababab', 'ab']
print(re.findall(r"(ab)+", "ababab xab"))            # ['ab', 'ab']   ← only the last repetition
SyntaxMeaning
(…)Capturing group — m.group(1)
(?P<name>…)Named group — m['name'], m.groupdict()
(?:…)Non-capturing group — groups for | or quantifiers without capturing
\1, (?P=name)Backreference to what a group matched
Watch out

With groups in the pattern, findall returns the groups, not the whole match. Use (?:…) for grouping you don't want captured, or finditer + m.group(0).

Backreferences
Python
import re
print(re.findall(r"\b(\w+) \1\b", "this is is a test test"))   # ['is', 'test']
print(bool(re.fullmatch(r"(['\"]).*\1", "'quoted'")))           # True
print(re.sub(r"\b(\w+) \1\b", r"\1", "the the cat sat sat"))    # the cat sat
Substitute & split
Python
import re
print(re.sub(r"\s+", " ", "too    many\n spaces"))                 # too many spaces
print(re.sub(r"(\d{4})-(\d{2})-(\d{2})", r"\3/\2/\1", "2026-09-29"))   # 29/09/2026
