    except ZeroDivisionError:
        raise ValueError("bad ratio") from None    # hide the internal detail

def inspect(f):
    try:
        f()
    except ValueError as e:
        return type(e.__cause__).__name__, type(e.__context__).__name__, e.__suppress_context__

print(inspect(implicit))   # ('NoneType', 'ZeroDivisionError', False)
print(inspect(explicit))   # ('ZeroDivisionError', 'ZeroDivisionError', True)
print(inspect(hidden))     # ('NoneType', 'ZeroDivisionError', True)
You writeAttribute setTraceback says
raise New() inside except__context__“During handling of the above exception, another exception occurred”
raise New() from e__cause__ (+ __context__)“The above exception was the direct cause of the following exception”
raise New() from None__suppress_context__ = TrueOnly the new exception
Adding context: add_note (3.11+)
Python
import traceback

def parse_row(n, text):
    try:
        return int(text)
    except ValueError as e:
        e.add_note(f"while parsing row {n} of users.csv")
        raise

try:
    parse_row(3, "abc")
except ValueError as e:
    print(e.__notes__)                                       # ['while parsing row 3 of users.csv']
    print(traceback.format_exception_only(e)[-1].strip())    # while parsing row 3 of users.csv

Notes are printed under the error message in tracebacks — a lightweight way to add context without wrapping the exception in a new type.

ExceptionGroup and except* (3.11+)

When several independent operations fail at once (parallel tasks, validating many fields), raise them together as an ExceptionGroup. except* handles each type inside the group; several except* clauses can run for one group.

Python
def validate(user):
    errors = []
    if not user.get("name"):
        errors.append(ValueError("name is required"))
    if not isinstance(user.get("age"), int):
        errors.append(TypeError("age must be an int"))
    if "@" not in user.get("email", ""):
        errors.append(ValueError("email is invalid"))
    if errors:
        raise ExceptionGroup("invalid user", errors)

try:
    validate({"name": "", "age": "x", "email": "nope"})
except* ValueError as eg:
    print("values:", [str(e) for e in eg.exceptions])   # values: ['name is required', 'email is invalid']
except* TypeError as eg:
    print("types:", [str(e) for e in eg.exceptions])    # types: ['age must be an int']
Python
eg = ExceptionGroup("batch", [ValueError(1), KeyError(2), ValueError(3)])
values, rest = eg.split(ValueError)
print(len(values.exceptions), [type(e).__name__ for e in rest.exceptions])   # 2 ['KeyError']
Key point

asyncio.TaskGroup raises an ExceptionGroup when tasks fail, so except* is the natural way to handle concurrent failures.

Inspecting tracebacks
Python
import traceback

def inner():
    return 1 / 0
def outer():
    return inner()

try:
    outer()
except ZeroDivisionError as e:
    frames = traceback.extract_tb(e.__traceback__)
    print([f.name for f in frames])                          # ['<module>', 'outer', 'inner']
    print(traceback.format_exception_only(e)[0].strip())     # ZeroDivisionError: division by zero

traceback.format_exc() returns the full traceback as a string (for logging); logger.exception("msg") does the same automatically inside an except block.

assert — for invariants, not validation
Python
def mean(xs):
    assert xs, "mean() of an empty list"     # a developer's sanity check
    return sum(xs) / len(xs)

try:
    mean([])
except AssertionError as e:
    print(e)                                 # mean() of an empty list
Watch out

python -O strips every assert. Never use it to check user input, permissions or anything that must hold in production — raise ValueError instead. And assert (cond, "msg") is always true (a non-empty tuple); Python emits a SyntaxWarning for it.

Warnings

A warning reports something suspicious without stopping the program — most often a deprecation. Warnings are printed once per location by default and can be filtered or turned into errors.

Python
import warnings

def old_api():
    warnings.warn("old_api() is deprecated; use new_api()", DeprecationWarning, stacklevel=2)
    return 42

with warnings.catch_warnings(record=True) as caught:
    warnings.simplefilter("always")
    result = old_api()

print(result, caught[0].category.__name__)   # 42 DeprecationWarning
print(caught[0].message)                     # old_api() is deprecated; use new_api()
Filter actionEffect
"default"Print the first occurrence per location
"always"Print every occurrence
"ignore"Never print
"error"Turn the warning into an exception — great in test suites (python -W error, pytest -W error)
finally beats return
Python
def f():
    try:
        return "from try"
    finally:
        print("finally runs first")   # finally runs first

print(f())                            # from try
Python
import warnings

# A return inside finally silently DISCARDS an in-flight exception.
# Python 3.14 warns about it at compile time (PEP 765); silenced here to show the behaviour.
src = """
def swallow():
    try:
        raise ValueError("lost!")
    finally:
        return "finally wins"
"""
with warnings.catch_warnings():
    warnings.simplefilter("ignore", SyntaxWarning)
    ns = {}
    exec(src, ns)
print(ns["swallow"]())      # finally wins
Watch out

Never return, break or continue out of a finally block — the exception being propagated vanishes without a trace.

EAFP, cost and the retry pattern

Setting up a try block is essentially free in Python 3.11+ (“zero-cost” exceptions); raising costs a few microseconds. So EAFP — just try and handle failure — is idiomatic when failure is the exception, not the rule.

Python
def get_port(cfg):
    try:
        return int(cfg["port"])
    except (KeyError, ValueError):
        return 8080

print(get_port({"port": "5432"}), get_port({}), get_port({"port": "x"}))   # 5432 8080 8080
Python — retry with exponential backoff
import time

def retry(fn, attempts=3, base_delay=0.001, retry_on=(ConnectionError, TimeoutError)):
    for attempt in range(1, attempts + 1):
        try:
            return fn()
        except retry_on:
            if attempt == attempts:
                raise                                    # out of attempts: propagate
            time.sleep(base_delay * 2 ** (attempt - 1))  # exponential backoff

calls = []
def flaky():
    calls.append(1)
    if len(calls) < 3:
        raise ConnectionError("connection reset")
    return "connected"

print(retry(flaky), len(calls))   # connected 3
Do

Retry only transient errors (timeouts, connection resets), cap the attempts, back off exponentially (add jitter in production) and log each failure.

Avoid

Retrying everything — a ValueError from bad input will fail identically every time.

Interview questions
Why shouldn't you write except: or except BaseException:?

They catch KeyboardInterrupt and SystemExit too, so Ctrl-C and sys.exit() stop working, and they hide real bugs. Catch Exception at most (e.g. at a top-level request handler that logs and continues), and specific types everywhere else.

What is the difference between raise X from e and raise X from None?

from e records e as the explicit cause (__cause__) and the traceback shows both, linked by “direct cause”. from None suppresses the context so only the new exception is shown — useful when the original is an implementation detail.

What does except* do?

It matches exceptions inside an ExceptionGroup. Each except* clause receives a sub-group of the matching exceptions; multiple clauses can run for the same group, and any unmatched exceptions are re-raised in a new group.

When would you define a custom exception?

When callers need to react to a specific domain failure (CardDeclined, RateLimited) differently from others. Give the library one base class so callers can catch all of its errors, and attach structured data as attributes.

Errors, Modules & Files

Modules, Packages & Imports

A module is a .py file; a package is a folder of modules. import finds, runs and caches them — understanding that cycle explains __name__, circular imports and sys.modules.

Importing
Python
import math                          # whole module
from collections import Counter      # one name
import datetime as dt                # alias
print(math.sqrt(16), Counter("aab")["a"], dt.date(2026, 1, 1).year)   # 4.0 2 2026
1
Check the cache
If the name is already in sys.modules, that module object is returned immediately.
2
Find
Otherwise Python searches sys.path: the script's folder, PYTHONPATH, the standard library, then site-packages.
3
Execute once
A new module object is created and the file's top-level code runs in its namespace.
4
Cache & bind
The module is stored in sys.modules and the name is bound in the importing namespace.
FormBindsUse when
import jsonjsonDefault — the call site shows where names come from (json.dumps)
import numpy as npnpConventional short aliases
from pathlib import PathPathNames you use a lot
from os import *Every public nameAlmost never — pollutes the namespace and hides origins
Imports run once and are cached
Python
import importlib, sys, tempfile
from pathlib import Path

folder = tempfile.mkdtemp()
Path(folder, "lvn_greet.py").write_text('print("module body runs")\nVALUE = 1\n')
sys.path.insert(0, folder)

import lvn_greet                  # runs the file: prints "module body runs"
import lvn_greet                  # cached in sys.modules: nothing printed
print("lvn_greet" in sys.modules, lvn_greet.VALUE)

lvn_greet.VALUE = 99
importlib.reload(lvn_greet)       # re-executes the file (prints again), resetting VALUE
print(lvn_greet.VALUE)

sys.path.remove(folder)
del sys.modules["lvn_greet"]
Output
module body runs
True 1
module body runs
1
Key point

Because a module runs once, module-level variables act like singletons — every importer shares the same object. It's also why top-level side effects (connecting to a DB, printing) happen at import time: keep them out of importable modules.

if __name__ == "__main__":
Python
# utils.py
def add(a, b):
    return a + b

if __name__ == "__main__":    # True only when run directly: python utils.py
    print(add(2, 3))          # 5
Key point

When the file is imported, __name__ is "utils", so the demo code doesn't run. Use this guard for scripts, tests and anything with side effects.

Module attributes
Python
import json, os

print(json.__name__, os.path.__name__)                   # json posixpath
print(json.__file__.endswith("json/__init__.py"))        # True   ← json is a package
print(json.__doc__.splitlines()[0])                      # JSON (JavaScript Object Notation) <https://json.org> is a subset of
print(__name__)                                          # __main__
AttributeHolds
__name__The module's import name ("__main__" for the script being run)
__file__Path of the source file (absent for built-in modules like sys)
__doc__The module docstring — the first string literal in the file
__all__Names exported by from module import *
__path__Only on packages: where to look for submodules
__all__ and import *
Python
import sys, tempfile
from pathlib import Path

folder = tempfile.mkdtemp()
Path(folder, "lvn_shapes.py").write_text(
    "__all__ = ['area']\n"
    "def area(r): return 3 * r * r\n"
    "def debug(): pass\n"
    "def _helper(): pass\n"
)
sys.path.insert(0, folder)
from lvn_shapes import *
print("area" in dir(), "debug" in dir(), "_helper" in dir())   # True False False
sys.path.remove(folder)

Without __all__, import * brings in every name that doesn't start with _. With it, only the listed names — __all__ is how a module declares its public API.

Packages
Layout
shop/
├── __init__.py        # marks a regular package; runs on "import shop"
├── __main__.py        # runs on "python -m shop"
├── cart.py
└── payments/
    ├── __init__.py
    └── upi.py

from shop.cart import Cart            # absolute import (preferred)
from .cart import Cart                # relative import (only inside the package)
from ..payments import upi            # two dots = parent package

__init__.py

Runs when the package is imported. Often empty, or re-exports the public API: from .cart import Cart.

Namespace packages

A folder without __init__.py still imports (PEP 420) and can be split across several directories — used by big plugin ecosystems. Prefer regular packages for your own code.

Relative imports

from . import x works only inside a package; running such a file directly (python shop/cart.py) fails — use python -m shop.cart.

python -m pkg.mod

Runs a module as a script with the right sys.path. python -m http.server, python -m json.tool, python -m venv, python -m pip.

Importing by name: importlib
Python
import importlib

mod = importlib.import_module("json")                     # module name as a string
print(mod.dumps([1, 2]))                                  # [1, 2]

plugin = "collections"                                    # e.g. read from a config file
Counter = getattr(importlib.import_module(plugin), "Counter")
print(Counter("aab")["a"])                                # 2
Circular imports

a.py imports b, and b.py imports a. The second import finds a half-initialised in sys.modules, so names defined later in a don't exist yet.

Typical error
ImportError: cannot import name 'User' from partially initialized module 'models'
(most likely due to a circular import)
  1. Restructure: move the shared code into a third module both can import (usually the real fix).
  2. Import the module, not the name: import models and use models.User at call time.
  3. Move the import inside the function that needs it (deferred import).
  4. For type hints only, guard with if typing.TYPE_CHECKING: and use string annotations.
Virtual environments

Each project should install its dependencies into its own virtual environment — see Virtual Environments, pip & Packaging for the full workflow.

Terminal
$ python3 -m venv .venv && source .venv/bin/activate
(.venv) $ pip install requests
What happens when you import a module twice?

The first import finds, executes and caches it in sys.modules; the second just returns the cached module object — the file doesn't run again. importlib.reload forces re-execution.

How do you fix a circular import?

Best: move the shared pieces into a separate module. Otherwise import the module rather than names from it, defer the import into a function, or use TYPE_CHECKING for imports needed only by type hints.

Errors, Modules & Files

Virtual Environments, pip & Packaging

Isolate each project's dependencies in a virtual environment, pin them for reproducible installs, and describe your own project in pyproject.toml so it can be installed and published.

Virtual environment — a directory with its own python executable and site-packages. Packages installed while it's active go there, not into the system Python — so two projects can use different versions of the same library.

The venv lifecycle
Terminal
$ python3 -m venv .venv                   # create (once per project; don't commit it)
$ source .venv/bin/activate               # activate — Windows: .venv\Scripts\activate
(.venv) $ which python                    # → /path/to/project/.venv/bin/python
(.venv) $ python -m pip install --upgrade pip
(.venv) $ pip install "requests>=2.31" pytest
(.venv) $ deactivate                      # back to the system Python
$ rm -rf .venv                            # environments are disposable: delete & recreate
Python
import sys
print("inside a venv:", sys.prefix != sys.base_prefix)   # True inside a venv, False otherwise
print(sys.version_info >= (3, 10))                      # True
Key point

Activation only prepends .venv/bin to PATH. You can skip it entirely by calling the interpreter directly: .venv/bin/python -m pytest. Add .venv/ to .gitignore.

pip essentials
CommandDoes
pip install pkgInstall the latest compatible version (plus dependencies)
pip install "pkg==1.4.2"Install an exact version
pip install -U pkgUpgrade
pip uninstall pkgRemove (dependencies stay installed)
pip list / pip list --outdatedWhat's installed / what can be upgraded
pip show pkgVersion, location, dependencies of one package
pip freeze > requirements.txtExact versions of everything installed
pip install -r requirements.txtInstall from a requirements file
pip install -e .Editable install of the current project
Watch out

Prefer python -m pip … over bare pip: it guarantees you're installing into the interpreter you think you are. And never sudo pip install into the system Python.

Version specifiers
SpecifierMeans
==1.4.2Exactly 1.4.2
>=1.4,<2At least 1.4, below 2.0
~=1.4.2Compatible release: >=1.4.2, ==1.4.*
~=1.4>=1.4, ==1.* (i.e. below 2.0)
!=1.5.0Anything except 1.5.0
requests[socks]Install with an optional extra
tomli; python_version < "3.11"Environment marker — only on older Pythons
requirements.txt vs lock files

Library

Declares loose ranges (requests>=2.28) in pyproject.toml so it can coexist with other packages.

Application

Pins exact versions of every package, including transitive ones, for reproducible deploys.

requirements.txt

A plain list, often produced by pip freeze or pip-compile. No hashes by default.

Lock files

uv.lock, poetry.lock, pylock.toml (PEP 751): exact versions + hashes for every platform, generated from your declared ranges.

pyproject.toml
pyproject.toml
[project]
name = "lvn-notes"
version = "0.3.0"
description = "Study notes toolkit"
readme = "README.md"
requires-python = ">=3.10"
dependencies = ["requests>=2.31", "rich~=13.7"]

[project.optional-dependencies]
dev = ["pytest>=8", "mypy", "ruff"]

[project.scripts]
lvn = "lvn_notes.cli:main"          # installs an `lvn` command

[build-system]
requires = ["hatchling"]
build-backend = "hatchling.build"

[tool.ruff]
line-length = 100

It's plain TOML, so the standard library can read it:

Python
import tomllib

text = """
[project]
name = "lvn-notes"
requires-python = ">=3.10"
dependencies = ["requests>=2.31", "rich~=13.7"]
[project.scripts]
lvn = "lvn_notes.cli:main"
"""
meta = tomllib.loads(text)["project"]
print(meta["name"], meta["dependencies"])   # lvn-notes ['requests>=2.31', 'rich~=13.7']
print(tomllib.loads(text)["project"]["scripts"]["lvn"])   # lvn_notes.cli:main
Project layout, editable installs & publishing
src layout
lvn-notes/
├── pyproject.toml
├── README.md
├── src/
│   └── lvn_notes/
│       ├── __init__.py
│       └── cli.py
└── tests/
    └── test_cli.py
Terminal
(.venv) $ pip install -e ".[dev]"      # editable: code changes apply without reinstalling
(.venv) $ python -m pip install build twine
(.venv) $ python -m build              # → dist/lvn_notes-0.3.0.tar.gz and .whl
(.venv) $ twine upload dist/*          # publish to PyPI (use TestPyPI first)
Key point

The src/ layout stops tests from accidentally importing the package from the working directory — they must use the installed version, exactly like your users.

ArtifactWhat it is
sdist (.tar.gz)Source distribution — built on the user's machine
wheel (.whl)Pre-built zip that installs by just unpacking — fast; platform-specific if it contains C extensions
The tool landscape
ToolRole
venv + pipBuilt in. Enough for most projects
uvVery fast Rust replacement for pip / venv / pip-tools; manages Python versions and uv.lock (uv add, uv run)
PoetryDependency management + lock file + build/publish in one tool
pipxInstalls CLI applications (black, httpie) each in its own isolated venv
condaEnvironments with non-Python binaries too (CUDA, MKL) — popular in data science
pyenvInstall and switch between several Python versions
pip-toolspip-compile turns loose requirements.in into pinned requirements.txt
Why use a virtual environment?

To isolate each project's dependencies: different projects can need different versions of the same library, and installing into the system Python can break OS tools. A venv also makes the dependency list explicit and reproducible.

What is the difference between pip freeze output and pyproject.toml dependencies?

pyproject.toml lists the direct dependencies with compatible ranges — what your code needs. pip freeze lists every installed package at its exact version — a snapshot for reproducing one environment.

What does pip install -e . do?

An editable (development) install: the package is registered in the environment but points at your source folder, so edits take effect immediately without reinstalling.

Errors, Modules & Files

Files & pathlib

Reading and writing files safely with with, text vs binary modes, seeking and chunked reads, pathlib for paths, and shutil / tempfile for everything around them.

Text files
Python
import os, tempfile
path = os.path.join(tempfile.mkdtemp(), "notes.txt")

with open(path, "w", encoding="utf-8") as f:     # "w" truncates, "a" appends
    f.write("line one\nline two\n")

with open(path, encoding="utf-8") as f:           # default mode "r"
    for line in f:                                # streams line by line
        print(line.rstrip())
Output
line one
line two
ModeMeaning
'r'Read (default) — error if missing
'w'Write — creates or truncates
'a'Append to the end
'x'Create — error if it exists
'b'Binary, e.g. 'rb' for images
'+'Read and write
Watch out

Always pass encoding="utf-8" for text — the platform default differs between Windows and macOS/Linux.

Reading methods
Python
import io
f = io.StringIO("alpha\nbeta\ngamma\n")    # behaves like an open text file

print(f.readline())            # alpha
print(f.read(3))               # bet
print(f.readlines())           # ['a\n', 'gamma\n']
print(repr(f.read()))          # ''   ← at the end, read() returns an empty string
Output
alpha

bet
['a\n', 'gamma\n']
''
CallReturnsMemory
for line in fOne line at a timeO(1) — best for big files
f.read()The whole file as one stringWhole file
f.read(n)Up to n characters (bytes in binary mode)O(n)
f.readline()Next line, including \nOne line
f.readlines()List of all linesWhole file
f.write(s) / f.writelines(lines)Write text (no newline added)—
Binary files, chunks & seek/tell
Python
import os, tempfile
path = os.path.join(tempfile.mkdtemp(), "data.bin")

with open(path, "wb") as f:
    f.write(bytes(range(10)))

chunks = []
with open(path, "rb") as f:
    while chunk := f.read(4):             # fixed-size chunks: constant memory
        chunks.append(chunk)
print(chunks)                             # [b'\x00\x01\x02\x03', b'\x04\x05\x06\x07', b'\x08\t']

with open(path, "rb") as f:
    f.seek(6)                             # jump to byte offset 6
    print(f.tell(), f.read(2))            # 6 b'\x06\x07'
    f.seek(-1, os.SEEK_END)               # 1 byte before the end
    print(f.read())                       # b'\t'
Key point

In text mode, seek only accepts offsets previously returned by tell() (or 0) because characters can be several bytes. Use binary mode for random access. Hashing a big file? Read it in chunks and call h.update(chunk).

Temporary files & directories
Python
import os, tempfile

with tempfile.TemporaryDirectory() as d:          # deleted with everything inside
    path = os.path.join(d, "scratch.txt")
    with open(path, "w", encoding="utf-8") as f:
        f.write("draft")
    print(os.path.exists(path))                   # True
print(os.path.exists(d))                          # False

with tempfile.NamedTemporaryFile("w+", suffix=".txt", encoding="utf-8") as tmp:
    tmp.write("scratch")
    tmp.seek(0)                                   # rewind before reading back
    print(tmp.read(), tmp.name.endswith(".txt"))  # scratch True
pathlib
Python
from pathlib import Path
import tempfile

root = Path(tempfile.mkdtemp())
report = root / "reports" / "2026.txt"      # "/" joins paths
report.parent.mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=True)
report.write_text("ok", encoding="utf-8")

print(report.name, report.stem, report.suffix)   # 2026.txt 2026 .txt
print(report.read_text(encoding="utf-8"))         # ok
print([p.name for p in root.rglob("*.txt")])      # ['2026.txt']
print(report.relative_to(root).as_posix())        # reports/2026.txt
print(report.with_suffix(".md").name)             # 2026.md
Taskos / os.pathpathlib
Joinos.path.join(a, b)Path(a) / b
File name / extensionos.path.basename(p), os.path.splitext(p)p.name, p.stem, p.suffix
Parent folderos.path.dirname(p)p.parent
Exists / is fileos.path.exists(p), os.path.isfile(p)p.exists(), p.is_file()
Make foldersos.makedirs(p, exist_ok=True)p.mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=True)
List / pattern matchos.listdir(p), glob.glob('*.py')p.iterdir(), p.glob('*.py'), p.rglob('*.py')
Read whole fileopen(p).read()p.read_text(encoding=…), p.read_bytes()
Absolute pathos.path.abspath(p)p.resolve()
Key point

Prefer pathlib in new code — paths become objects with methods instead of strings passed to functions. Every standard-library function that takes a path also accepts a Path.

glob & shutil: finding, copying, moving
Python
import glob, os, shutil, tempfile
from pathlib import Path

with tempfile.TemporaryDirectory() as d:
    root = Path(d)
    for name in ["a.py", "b.py", "notes.md"]:
        (root / name).write_text(name, encoding="utf-8")

    print(sorted(p.name for p in root.glob("*.py")))                          # ['a.py', 'b.py']
    print(sorted(os.path.basename(p) for p in glob.glob(str(root / "*.md"))))  # ['notes.md']

    backup = root / "backup"
    backup.mkdir()
    shutil.copy(root / "a.py", backup)                  # copy into a folder
    shutil.move(root / "notes.md", backup / "README.md")  # move + rename
    print(sorted(p.name for p in backup.iterdir()))     # ['README.md', 'a.py']

    shutil.rmtree(backup)                               # delete a whole tree
    print(backup.exists())                              # False
Safe writes
Python
import os, tempfile
from pathlib import Path

def atomic_write(path: Path, text: str) -> None:
    """Readers see either the old file or the new one — never half a file."""
    tmp = path.with_suffix(path.suffix + ".tmp")
    tmp.write_text(text, encoding="utf-8")
    os.replace(tmp, path)            # atomic rename on the same filesystem

target = Path(tempfile.mkdtemp()) / "settings.json"
atomic_write(target, '{"theme": "dark"}')
print(target.read_text(encoding="utf-8"))   # {"theme": "dark"}
Do

Use with open(...), pass encoding="utf-8", stream big files line by line or in chunks, and use tempfile for scratch files.

Avoid

Calling open() without closing it, read() on multi-GB files, or building paths with string + (folder + "/" + name).

Structured formats — JSON, CSV, pickle, TOML — are covered in Serialization: JSON, CSV & pickle.

How would you process a 10 GB log file?

Stream it: with open(path, encoding='utf-8') as f: for line in f: keeps one line in memory at a time. For binary data read fixed-size chunks with f.read(1 << 20). Combine with generators to build a lazy pipeline.

Errors, Modules & Files

Serialization: JSON, CSV & pickle

Turning Python objects into bytes or text and back — JSON for APIs, CSV for tables, TOML/INI for config, pickle for Python-only caches, and struct for binary formats.

FormatModuleHuman-readableCross-languageSafe on untrusted input
JSONjsonYesYesYes
CSVcsvYesYesYes
TOMLtomllib (read-only)YesYesYes
INIconfigparserYesMostlyYes
picklepickleNoPython onlyNo — can run code
SQLitesqlite3No (queryable)YesYes
Packed binarystructNoYes (you define the layout)Yes
JSON basics & type mapping
Python
import json
user = {"name": "Asha", "skills": ["python", "sql"], "active": True, "manager": None}
text = json.dumps(user)                  # object → str
print(text)                              # {"name": "Asha", "skills": ["python", "sql"], "active": true, "manager": null}
print(json.loads(text)["skills"][0])     # python

print(json.loads(json.dumps({1: (1, 2)})))   # {'1': [1, 2]}   ← int keys become strings, tuples become lists
PythonJSONBack to Python
dictobject {}dict (keys always str)
list, tuplearray []list
strstringstr
int, floatnumberint / float
True / Falsetrue / falsebool
NonenullNone
set, datetime, Decimal, objectsTypeError— convert first
Key point

dumps / loads work with strings; dump(obj, f) / load(f) with open files.

Formatting options
Python
import json
data = {"name": "Zoë", "tags": ["a", "b"], "id": 7}

print(json.dumps(data))                                         # {"name": "Zo\u00eb", "tags": ["a", "b"], "id": 7}
print(json.dumps(data, ensure_ascii=False, sort_keys=True))     # {"id": 7, "name": "Zoë", "tags": ["a", "b"]}
print(json.dumps(data, ensure_ascii=False, separators=(",", ":")))   # {"name":"Zoë","tags":["a","b"],"id":7}
Python
import json
print(json.dumps({"id": 7, "tags": ["a", "b"]}, indent=2))
Output
{
  "id": 7,
  "tags": [
    "a",
    "b"
  ]
}
OptionEffect
indent=2Pretty-print (config files, debugging)
sort_keys=TrueStable key order — diff-friendly output, cache keys
ensure_ascii=FalseKeep non-ASCII characters instead of \uXXXX escapes
separators=(',', ':')Most compact output (network payloads)
default=fnCalled for objects JSON can't encode
allow_nan=FalseReject NaN/Infinity, which are not valid JSON
Custom types: default and object_hook
Python
import json
from dataclasses import dataclass, asdict, is_dataclass
from datetime import date

@dataclass
class Event:
    title: str
    day: date
    tags: set

def encode(obj):
    if isinstance(obj, date):
        return obj.isoformat()
    if isinstance(obj, set):
        return sorted(obj)
    if is_dataclass(obj):
        return asdict(obj)
    raise TypeError(f"{type(obj).__name__} is not JSON serializable")

text = json.dumps(Event("launch", date(2026, 9, 29), {"b", "a"}), default=encode)
print(text)                                  # {"title": "launch", "day": "2026-09-29", "tags": ["a", "b"]}

def decode(d):                               # called for every JSON object, innermost first
    if "day" in d:
        d["day"] = date.fromisoformat(d["day"])
    return d

back = json.loads(text, object_hook=decode)
print(back["day"].year, type(back["day"]).__name__)   # 2026 date
Python
import json
try:
    json.loads("{'single': 'quotes'}")        # Python-style dict text is NOT JSON
except json.JSONDecodeError as e:
    print(e.msg, "at position", e.pos)        # Expecting property name enclosed in double quotes at position 1
CSV: reading & writing
Python
import csv, io

rows = [{"name": "Asha", "city": "Pune, MH"}, {"name": "Ravi", "city": 'Say "hi"'}]
buf = io.StringIO()
writer = csv.DictWriter(buf, fieldnames=["name", "city"])
writer.writeheader()
writer.writerows(rows)
print(buf.getvalue().splitlines())   # ['name,city', 'Asha,"Pune, MH"', 'Ravi,"Say ""hi"""']   ← commas and quotes are escaped for you

reader = csv.reader(io.StringIO(buf.getvalue()))
header = next(reader)
print(header, list(reader))       # ['name', 'city'] [['Asha', 'Pune, MH'], ['Ravi', 'Say "hi"']]

raw = "name,score\nasha,91\nravi,84\n"
scores = [int(r["score"]) for r in csv.DictReader(io.StringIO(raw))]
print(sum(scores) / len(scores))  # 87.5
Python
import csv, io
tsv = "a\tb\n1\t2\n"
print(list(csv.reader(io.StringIO(tsv), delimiter="\t")))    # [['a', 'b'], ['1', '2']]

buf = io.StringIO()
csv.writer(buf, quoting=csv.QUOTE_NONNUMERIC, lineterminator="\n").writerow(["x", 1.5, 2])
print(buf.getvalue().strip())                                # "x",1.5,2
Watch out

Open CSV files with open(path, "w", newline="", encoding="utf-8"). Without newline="" you get blank lines between rows on Windows. And never split lines on , by hand — quoted fields contain commas.

Key point

Every CSV value is read as a string — convert types yourself. For heavy tabular work use pandas (pd.read_csv).

pickle — Python objects as bytes
Python
import pickle

data = {"ids": (1, 2, 3), "tags": {"py"}, "nested": [b"raw", None, 3.5]}
blob = pickle.dumps(data)                    # tuples, sets, bytes: all preserved
restored = pickle.loads(blob)
print(type(blob).__name__, restored == data, type(restored["ids"]).__name__)   # bytes True tuple
Security

Never unpickle data from an untrusted source. A pickle can instruct the loader to call any function (via __reduce__), i.e. run arbitrary code. Use JSON for anything that crosses a trust boundary.

How it stores classes

By import path (module.ClassName), not code — the class must be importable when loading, and renaming it breaks old pickles.

Good uses

Caching intermediate results, passing objects between your own processes (multiprocessing uses pickle).

Bad uses

Long-term storage, APIs, anything shared across Python versions or teams.

shelve

A persistent dict backed by pickle: with shelve.open('cache') as db: db['k'] = obj.

sqlite3 — a database in the standard library
Python
import sqlite3

con = sqlite3.connect(":memory:")            # or a file path
con.execute("CREATE TABLE users (id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY, name TEXT)")
with con:                                    # commits on success, rolls back on error
    con.executemany("INSERT INTO users (name) VALUES (?)", [("Asha",), ("Ravi",)])
print(con.execute("SELECT id, name FROM users ORDER BY id").fetchall())   # [(1, 'Asha'), (2, 'Ravi')]
print(con.execute("SELECT name FROM users WHERE id = ?", (2,)).fetchone())  # ('Ravi',)
con.close()
Watch out

Always pass values as parameters (?), never with f-strings — string-built SQL is an injection hole.

Config files: TOML & INI
Python
import tomllib, configparser

cfg = tomllib.loads("""
[server]
host = "0.0.0.0"
port = 8080
debug = false

[database]
pool = { min = 1, max = 5 }
""")
print(cfg["server"]["port"], cfg["server"]["debug"], cfg["database"]["pool"]["max"])   # 8080 False 5

cp = configparser.ConfigParser()
cp.read_string("""
[app]
name = notes
workers = 4
debug = yes
""")
print(cp["app"]["name"], cp.getint("app", "workers"), cp.getboolean("app", "debug"))   # notes 4 True
Key point

TOML has real types (ints, booleans, dates, arrays); INI values are all strings until you call getint / getboolean. tomllib (3.11+) only reads — write TOML with the third-party tomli-w. Use tomllib.load with a file opened in binary mode.

Binary layouts with struct
Python
import struct

# "<" little-endian, H = unsigned 16-bit, I = unsigned 32-bit, f = 32-bit float
packed = struct.pack("<HIf", 7, 65536, 1.5)
print(len(packed), packed.hex())             # 10 0700000001000000c03f
print(struct.unpack("<HIf", packed))         # (7, 65536, 1.5)
print(struct.calcsize("<HIf"), struct.calcsize("HIf"))   # 10 12   ← native mode adds alignment padding
JSON vs pickle — when would you use each?

JSON for data exchanged with other programs or stored long-term: it's text, language-neutral and safe to parse. Pickle only for short-lived, trusted, Python-to-Python data (caches, IPC) — it handles almost any Python object but is insecure and version-fragile.

How do you serialize a datetime or a custom object to JSON?

Pass default= to json.dumps — a function that converts unsupported objects (e.g. dt.isoformat(), dataclasses.asdict(obj)) — or subclass json.JSONEncoder. Decode with object_hook= to turn the dicts back into rich types.

Standard Library

Dates & Times (datetime)

datetime models calendar dates, clock times and durations. The golden rule: store and compute in UTC with time-zone-aware objects, convert to local time only for display.

The four core types
TypeHoldsExample
dateYear, month, daydate(2026, 9, 29)
timeHour … microsecond (+ optional tz)time(14, 5)
datetimeA date and a time (+ optional tz)datetime(2026, 9, 29, 14, 5)
timedeltaA duration: days, seconds, microsecondstimedelta(hours=36)
Python
from datetime import date, datetime, timedelta

d = date(2026, 9, 29)
print(d.year, d.month, d.day, d.weekday())         # 2026 9 29 1   ← Monday is 0
print(d.isoformat(), d.isocalendar().week)         # 2026-09-29 40

dt = datetime(2026, 9, 29, 14, 5)
print(dt.date(), dt.time())                        # 2026-09-29 14:05:00
print(dt.replace(hour=9, minute=0))                # 2026-09-29 09:00:00
Key point

date.today() and datetime.now() depend on when and where the code runs — fine in programs, but never hard-code their output in tests. Pass a fixed datetime (or a clock function) into code you want to test.

Arithmetic with timedelta
Python
from datetime import date, datetime, timedelta

print(date(2026, 12, 25) - date(2026, 9, 29))          # 87 days, 0:00:00
print((date(2026, 12, 25) - date(2026, 9, 29)).days)   # 87

start = datetime(2026, 1, 31, 22, 30)
print(start + timedelta(days=1, hours=3))              # 2026-02-02 01:30:00
print(start + timedelta(days=30))                      # 2026-03-02 22:30:00   ← no "add a month" in timedelta

span = timedelta(hours=36, minutes=15)
print(span, span.total_seconds())                      # 1 day, 12:15:00 130500.0
print(datetime(2026, 1, 1) < datetime(2026, 6, 1))     # True
Watch out

timedelta has no months or years (their length varies). For “same day next month”, compute it yourself or use dateutil.relativedelta.

Formatting & parsing: strftime / strptime
Python
from datetime import datetime

dt = datetime(2026, 9, 29, 14, 5, 9)
print(dt.strftime("%d/%m/%Y %H:%M"))              # 29/09/2026 14:05
print(dt.strftime("%A, %d %B %Y at %I:%M %p"))    # Tuesday, 29 September 2026 at 02:05 PM
print(f"{dt:%Y-%m-%d}")                           # 2026-09-29   ← format spec in an f-string

parsed = datetime.strptime("29-Sep-2026 18:30", "%d-%b-%Y %H:%M")
print(parsed)                                     # 2026-09-29 18:30:00
print(datetime.fromisoformat("2026-09-29T14:05:00+05:30").utcoffset())   # 5:30:00
CodeMeaningExample
%Y / %yYear, 4 / 2 digits2026 / 26
%m / %B / %bMonth number / full name / short name09 / September / Sep
%d / %jDay of month / day of year29 / 272
%H / %I / %pHour 24h / 12h / AM-PM14 / 02 / PM
%M / %S / %fMinute / second / microsecond05 / 09 / 000000
%A / %aWeekday full / shortTuesday / Tue
%z / %ZUTC offset / zone name+0530 / IST
Key point

Prefer ISO 8601 (2026-09-29T14:05:00+05:30) for storage and APIs: isoformat() and fromisoformat() round-trip it, and it sorts correctly as text.

Naive vs aware; time zones with zoneinfo

Naive vs aware — a naive datetime has no tzinfo — it's just wall-clock numbers. An aware one carries a UTC offset, so it denotes one exact instant.

Python
from datetime import datetime, timezone
from zoneinfo import ZoneInfo

utc = datetime(2026, 3, 8, 12, 0, tzinfo=timezone.utc)      # an exact instant
print(utc.astimezone(ZoneInfo("Asia/Kolkata")))             # 2026-03-08 17:30:00+05:30
print(utc.astimezone(ZoneInfo("America/New_York")))         # 2026-03-08 08:00:00-04:00   ← DST began that morning

meeting = datetime(2026, 9, 29, 9, 0, tzinfo=ZoneInfo("Europe/London"))
print(meeting.astimezone(timezone.utc))                     # 2026-09-29 08:00:00+00:00

naive = datetime(2026, 1, 1)
try:
    naive < utc
except TypeError:
    print("can't compare naive and aware")                  # can't compare naive and aware
Do

Use datetime.now(timezone.utc) and store UTC; convert with astimezone(ZoneInfo(...)) when displaying.

Avoid

datetime.utcnow() (deprecated since 3.12 — it returns a naive value that looks local) or storing local times without an offset.

Timestamps, time and calendar
Python
import time, calendar
from datetime import datetime, timezone

instant = datetime(2026, 9, 29, tzinfo=timezone.utc)
ts = instant.timestamp()                                   # seconds since 1970-01-01 UTC
print(ts)                                                  # 1790640000.0
print(datetime.fromtimestamp(ts, tz=timezone.utc))         # 2026-09-29 00:00:00+00:00

print(calendar.isleap(2028), calendar.monthrange(2026, 2)[1])   # True 28

t0 = time.perf_counter()                                   # for measuring durations
sum(range(10_000))
print(time.perf_counter() - t0 >= 0)                       # True
ClockUse for
time.time()Wall-clock timestamp (can jump if the system clock changes)
time.monotonic()Timeouts and intervals — never goes backwards
time.perf_counter()Benchmarking — highest resolution
time.sleep(s)Pause the current thread
What is the difference between a naive and an aware datetime?

A naive datetime has no time-zone information, so it can't be mapped to a single instant; an aware one has tzinfo (an offset), so comparisons and arithmetic across zones are correct. Python refuses to compare the two.

How should you store timestamps in a database or API?

As UTC — either an aware datetime / TIMESTAMP WITH TIME ZONE, or an ISO 8601 string with an offset (2026-09-29T08:00:00+00:00). Convert to the user's zone only when displaying.

Why not use time.time() to measure how long code takes?

It follows the system clock, which can be adjusted (NTP, manual changes). Use time.perf_counter() (or monotonic()), which only moves forward.

Standard Library

os, sys, shutil & subprocess

The modules that let a Python program talk to its environment: the interpreter (sys), the operating system (os), whole files and folders (shutil) and other programs (subprocess).

sys — the interpreter
Python
import sys

print(sys.version_info >= (3, 10))          # True
print(type(sys.argv).__name__)               # list   ← ["script.py", "arg1", ...]
print(isinstance(sys.path, list))            # True   ← where imports are searched
print(sys.maxsize == 2**63 - 1)              # True   ← on 64-bit builds
print(sys.getrecursionlimit())               # 1000
NameWhat it is
sys.argvCommand-line arguments; argv[0] is the script name
sys.exit(code)Exit by raising SystemExit — 0 means success
sys.stdin / stdout / stderrThe standard streams (file objects)
sys.pathModule search path
sys.modulesCache of already-imported modules
sys.platform'linux', 'darwin', 'win32', …
sys.executablePath of the running Python interpreter
sys.getsizeof(obj)Shallow size of an object in bytes
os — environment, paths, processes
Python
import os

os.environ["APP_MODE"] = "test"                     # visible to child processes too
print(os.environ.get("APP_MODE"))                   # test
print(os.environ.get("NOT_SET_ANYWHERE", "default"))  # default
print(os.getenv("NOT_SET_ANYWHERE") is None)        # True
print(os.path.join("data", "2026", "report.csv"))   # data/2026/report.csv
print(os.path.splitext("photo.tar.gz"))             # ('photo.tar', '.gz')
print(os.sep, os.cpu_count() > 0)                   # / True
del os.environ["APP_MODE"]
Watch out

os.environ["KEY"] raises KeyError when the variable is missing — use os.environ.get("KEY", default) for optional settings, and never print secrets pulled from the environment.

Walking a directory tree
Python
import os, tempfile

root = tempfile.mkdtemp()
for rel in ["src/app.py", "src/util.py", "docs/index.md", "README.md"]:
    path = os.path.join(root, rel)
    os.makedirs(os.path.dirname(path), exist_ok=True)
    open(path, "w").close()

print(sorted(os.listdir(root)))                      # ['README.md', 'docs', 'src']
with os.scandir(root) as entries:                    # faster: file type info included
    print(sorted(e.name for e in entries if e.is_dir()))   # ['docs', 'src']

found = []
for dirpath, dirnames, filenames in os.walk(root):
    found += [f for f in filenames if f.endswith(".py")]
print(sorted(found))                                 # ['app.py', 'util.py']
Key point

For new code, pathlib.Path (see Files, JSON & pathlib) is usually nicer: Path(root).rglob("*.py") replaces the os.walk loop.

shutil — high-level file operations
Python
import shutil, tempfile
from pathlib import Path

base = Path(tempfile.mkdtemp())
(base / "src").mkdir()
(base / "src" / "a.txt").write_text("hello")

shutil.copy(base / "src" / "a.txt", base / "b.txt")        # copy one file
shutil.copytree(base / "src", base / "backup")             # copy a whole tree
shutil.move(base / "b.txt", base / "c.txt")                # move / rename
shutil.rmtree(base / "src")                                # delete a tree — careful!
print(sorted(p.name for p in base.iterdir()))              # ['backup', 'c.txt']
print(shutil.which("definitely-not-a-real-cmd"))           # None
archive = shutil.make_archive(str(base / "bundle"), "zip", base / "backup")
print(Path(archive).name)                                  # bundle.zip
subprocess — running other programs
Python
import subprocess, sys

result = subprocess.run(
    [sys.executable, "-c", "print('hello from a child process')"],
    capture_output=True, text=True,
)
print(result.returncode, result.stdout.strip())   # 0 hello from a child process

try:
    subprocess.run([sys.executable, "-c", "raise SystemExit(2)"], check=True)
except subprocess.CalledProcessError as e:
    print("failed with", e.returncode)            # failed with 2

out = subprocess.run([sys.executable, "-c", "import sys; print(sys.stdin.read().upper())"],
                     input="piped text", capture_output=True, text=True, timeout=10)
print(out.stdout.strip())                          # PIPED TEXT
ArgumentEffect
["cmd", "arg"] (a list)Runs the program directly — no shell, no quoting problems
capture_output=TrueCollect stdout / stderr instead of printing them
text=TrueDecode bytes to str
check=TrueRaise CalledProcessError on a non-zero exit code
input=…Send data to the child's stdin
timeout=…Kill the child and raise TimeoutExpired if it takes too long
cwd=…, env=…Working directory / environment for the child
Shell injection

Never build a command string from user input with shell=True — f"ls {name}" with name = "x; rm -rf ~" runs both commands. Pass a list of arguments instead.

What is the difference between os and sys?

sys is about the Python interpreter itself — arguments, import path, standard streams, exit, recursion limit. os is about the operating system — environment variables, files and directories, processes, permissions.

How do you run a shell command from Python and get its output?

subprocess.run(["git", "status"], capture_output=True, text=True, check=True).stdout. Use a list of arguments (no shell=True), check=True to fail loudly, and timeout for anything that might hang.

How does sys.exit() work?

It raises SystemExit. If nothing catches it, the interpreter exits with that code (0 = success, non-zero = error; a string is printed to stderr and gives exit code 1). finally blocks and with exits still run.

Standard Library

Command-Line Scripts & argparse

Turning a Python file into a proper command-line tool: a main() entry point, arguments parsed with argparse, clean exit codes and input from files or stdin.

The script skeleton
Python
#!/usr/bin/env python3
"""wordcount — count words in text files."""
import argparse
import sys


def parse_args(argv=None):
    parser = argparse.ArgumentParser(description="Count words in text files.")
    parser.add_argument("files", nargs="+", help="files to read")
    parser.add_argument("-n", "--top", type=int, default=5, help="how many words to show")
    return parser.parse_args(argv)


def main(argv=None) -> int:
    args = parse_args(argv)
    ...                       # do the work
    return 0                  # exit code: 0 = success


if __name__ == "__main__":
    raise SystemExit(main())

Shebang

#!/usr/bin/env python3 + chmod +x wordcount.py lets you run ./wordcount.py directly on macOS/Linux.

main(argv=None)

Taking argv as a parameter makes the CLI testable: main(["a.txt", "-n", "3"]).

Exit codes

Return 0 on success and non-zero on failure — shells, CI and && chains depend on it.

Entry points

Packaged tools declare [project.scripts] wordcount = "pkg.cli:main" in pyproject.toml.

sys.argv — the raw arguments
Python
import sys

saved = sys.argv
sys.argv = ["greet.py", "Asha", "--loud"]    # what the shell would pass for: python greet.py Asha --loud
name = sys.argv[1]
loud = "--loud" in sys.argv[2:]
print(("HELLO " if loud else "hello ") + name)   # HELLO Asha
sys.argv = saved
Key point

sys.argv is fine for one or two arguments. As soon as you have options, defaults, types or --help, switch to argparse.

argparse — positional, optional, flags
Python
import argparse

parser = argparse.ArgumentParser(prog="resize")
parser.add_argument("image")                                      # positional, required
parser.add_argument("-w", "--width", type=int, required=True)     # option with a value
parser.add_argument("--format", choices=["png", "jpg"], default="png")
parser.add_argument("-q", "--quiet", action="store_true")         # flag: True if present
parser.add_argument("-v", "--verbose", action="count", default=0) # -vv → 2
parser.add_argument("--tag", action="append", default=[])         # repeatable
parser.add_argument("extra", nargs="*")                           # zero or more

args = parser.parse_args(["cat.png", "-w", "300", "-vv", "--tag", "a", "--tag", "b", "x", "y"])
print(args.image, args.width, args.format, args.quiet)   # cat.png 300 png False
print(args.verbose, args.tag, args.extra)                # 2 ['a', 'b'] ['x', 'y']
print(vars(args)["width"] * 2)                           # 600
OptionMeaning
type=int / float / Path / any callableConvert (and validate) the string
default=…Value when the option is absent
required=TrueMake an option mandatory
choices=[…]Restrict to allowed values
nargs="?" / "*" / "+" / 2Optional one / any number / at least one / exactly two
action="store_true"Boolean flag
action="count"Count repetitions (-vvv)
action="append"Collect repeated options into a list
dest="name", metavar="N"Attribute name / how the value is shown in help
Errors and --help for free
Python
import argparse, io, contextlib

parser = argparse.ArgumentParser(prog="resize")
parser.add_argument("-w", "--width", type=int, required=True)

err = io.StringIO()
try:
    with contextlib.redirect_stderr(err):
        parser.parse_args(["-w", "wide"])
except SystemExit as e:
    print("exit code", e.code)                          # exit code 2
print(err.getvalue().strip().splitlines()[-1])
# resize: error: argument -w/--width: invalid int value: 'wide'
Output
exit code 2
resize: error: argument -w/--width: invalid int value: 'wide'
Terminal
$ python resize.py --help
usage: resize [-h] -w WIDTH [--format {png,jpg}] [-q] [-v] image

positional arguments:
  image

options:
  -h, --help            show this help message and exit
  -w, --width WIDTH
  --format {png,jpg}
  -q, --quiet
  -v, --verbose
Subcommands (git-style)
Python
import argparse

parser = argparse.ArgumentParser(prog="todo")
sub = parser.add_subparsers(dest="command", required=True)

add = sub.add_parser("add", help="add a task")
add.add_argument("text")
add.add_argument("--priority", type=int, default=3)

done = sub.add_parser("done", help="complete a task")
done.add_argument("id", type=int)

print(vars(parser.parse_args(["add", "buy milk", "--priority", "1"])))
# {'command': 'add', 'text': 'buy milk', 'priority': 1}
print(vars(parser.parse_args(["done", "7"])))    # {'command': 'done', 'id': 7}
Output
{'command': 'add', 'text': 'buy milk', 'priority': 1}
{'command': 'done', 'id': 7}
Reading input: files or stdin
Python
import io, sys

def count_lines(stream) -> int:
    return sum(1 for line in stream if line.strip())

saved = sys.stdin
sys.stdin = io.StringIO("alpha\n\nbeta\ngamma\n")    # pretend: cat notes.txt | python count.py
print(count_lines(sys.stdin))                              # 3
sys.stdin = saved
Terminal
$ python count.py notes.txt            # read a file named on the command line
$ cat notes.txt | python count.py      # or read from a pipe
$ python count.py < notes.txt          # or from a redirect
$ python count.py notes.txt && echo ok # exit code 0 lets the chain continue
Key point

fileinput.input() iterates over the lines of every file named in sys.argv[1:], or stdin if there are none — the classic Unix-filter behaviour in one line.

click / typer

Third-party libraries that build CLIs from decorators or type hints — nicer for big tools.

rich

Colour, tables and progress bars in the terminal.

Why use raise SystemExit(main()) instead of just calling main()?

It turns main's return value into the process exit code, so shells and CI can tell success (0) from failure. Keeping logic in main(argv) also makes it importable and testable.

How does argparse report errors?

It prints a usage line and an error message to stderr and exits with status 2 (by raising SystemExit). --help prints help and exits with 0.

Standard Library

Logging

logging records what a program is doing — with severity levels, timestamps and destinations you can change without touching the code. It is how production code replaces print.

Why not print?
printlogging
SeverityNoneDEBUG → CRITICAL, filterable
Destinationstdout onlyConsole, files, syslog, HTTP… (handlers)
MetadataOnly what you typeTime, module, line, thread, level (formatters)
Turn off / onEdit the codeChange the level or config
LibrariesPollute the user's outputSilent unless the app configures logging
Levels
LevelValueUse for
DEBUG10Detailed diagnostics while developing
INFO20Normal events: started, user logged in, job finished
WARNING30Something unexpected but handled — the default threshold
ERROR40An operation failed
CRITICAL50The program may not be able to continue
Quick start
Python
import logging

logging.basicConfig(
    level=logging.INFO,
    format="%(asctime)s %(levelname)s %(name)s: %(message)s",
)
log = logging.getLogger(__name__)      # one logger per module

log.debug("not shown: below INFO")
log.info("server started on port %d", 8000)
log.warning("disk %s%% full", 91)
Output
2026-09-29 14:05:09,123 INFO __main__: server started on port 8000
2026-09-29 14:05:09,124 WARNING __main__: disk 91% full
Key point

basicConfig configures the root logger once, at the application's entry point. Library modules only call logging.getLogger(__name__) and log — they never configure handlers.

Loggers, handlers, formatters
Logger app.db
→
level filter
→
Handler(s)
→
Formatter
→
Output
Python
import io, logging

stream = io.StringIO()                                   # stands in for a console or file
handler = logging.StreamHandler(stream)
handler.setFormatter(logging.Formatter("%(levelname)-7s %(name)s | %(message)s"))

log = logging.getLogger("shop.orders")
log.setLevel(logging.DEBUG)
log.addHandler(handler)
log.propagate = False                                    # don't also send to the root logger

log.debug("cart has %d items", 3)
log.info("order %s placed", "A-17")
log.getChild("payments").error("card declined")          # child logger: shop.orders.payments

for line in stream.getvalue().splitlines():
    print(line)
Output
DEBUG   shop.orders | cart has 3 items
INFO    shop.orders | order A-17 placed
ERROR   shop.orders.payments | card declined

Logger

What you call (log.info). Named hierarchically with dots; children pass records up to parents.

Handler

Where records go: StreamHandler, FileHandler, RotatingFileHandler, TimedRotatingFileHandler, SysLogHandler…

Formatter

How a record looks: %(asctime)s, %(levelname)s, %(name)s, %(lineno)d, %(message)s…

Filter

Extra rules to drop or enrich records (e.g. add a request id).

Logging exceptions
Python
import io, logging

stream = io.StringIO()
log = logging.getLogger("demo.exc")
log.addHandler(logging.StreamHandler(stream))
log.propagate = False

try:
    1 / 0
except ZeroDivisionError:
    log.exception("could not compute ratio")      # ERROR level + full traceback

lines = stream.getvalue().splitlines()
print(lines[0])            # could not compute ratio
print(lines[1])            # Traceback (most recent call last):
print(lines[-1])           # ZeroDivisionError: division by zero
Configuring a real application
Python
import logging.config

logging.config.dictConfig({
    "version": 1,
    "formatters": {"plain": {"format": "%(asctime)s %(levelname)s %(name)s: %(message)s"}},
    "handlers": {
        "console": {"class": "logging.StreamHandler", "formatter": "plain", "level": "INFO"},
        "file": {"class": "logging.handlers.RotatingFileHandler", "formatter": "plain",
                 "filename": "app.log", "maxBytes": 1_000_000, "backupCount": 3},
    },
    "loggers": {"urllib3": {"level": "WARNING"}},          # quieten a chatty library
    "root": {"level": "DEBUG", "handlers": ["console", "file"]},
})
Best practices
Do

Pass arguments separately — log.info("user %s", uid) — so the string is only built if the record is actually emitted.

Avoid

log.info(f"user {uid}") in hot paths (always formatted), logging passwords or tokens, or except: log.error(e) without the traceback.

  • One logger per module: log = logging.getLogger(__name__).
  • Libraries add logging.NullHandler() to their top-level logger and never call basicConfig.
  • Use log.exception(...) inside except blocks to keep the traceback.
  • In services, log structured data (JSON lines) so log platforms can search fields — python-json-logger or structlog.
Why is logging.getLogger(__name__) the recommended pattern?

It creates a logger named after the module (shop.orders), so logger names mirror the package hierarchy. Configuration can then target a whole package (shop) or one module, and every record shows where it came from.

What is the default logging level?

WARNING. debug() and info() messages are dropped until you lower the level (e.g. basicConfig(level=logging.INFO)).

Why use %s arguments instead of f-strings in log calls?

Logging formats the message lazily — only if the record passes the level check. An f-string is built on every call even when the message is discarded. %-style arguments also let log aggregators group identical message templates.

Standard Library

Standard Library Tour

“Batteries included”: before installing a package, check the standard library. A map of the modules worth knowing, with a tiny example of each.

Where things live
NeedModule(s)Covered in
Containers & algorithmscollections, heapq, bisect, arrayData Structures
Iteration & functionsitertools, functools, operatorAdvanced Python, Functions
Textstring, re, textwrap, difflib, unicodedataStrings & Numbers
Numbersmath, decimal, fractions, statistics, randomNumbers, below
Files & formatspathlib, shutil, json, csv, tomllib, pickle, sqlite3, zipfileErrors, Modules & Files
Datesdatetime, zoneinfo, time, calendarDates & Times
OS & processesos, sys, subprocess, argparse, loggingthis group
Concurrencythreading, multiprocessing, concurrent.futures, asyncio, queueConcurrency
Testing & debuggingunittest, doctest, pdb, timeit, cProfile, tracemallocTesting & Tooling
