random & statistics
Python
import random, statistics

random.seed(42)                         # same seed → same sequence (reproducible experiments)
first = [random.randint(1, 6) for _ in range(5)]
random.seed(42)
print(first == [random.randint(1, 6) for _ in range(5)])   # True

rng = random.Random(7)                  # an independent generator with its own state
deck = list("AKQJ")
rng.shuffle(deck)                       # in place
print(len(rng.sample(range(100), 3)), rng.choice(deck) in deck)   # 3 True

data = [2, 4, 4, 4, 5, 5, 7, 9]
print(statistics.mean(data), statistics.median(data), statistics.mode(data))   # 5 4.5 4
print(statistics.pstdev(data), round(statistics.stdev(data), 3))             # 2.0 2.138
Watch out

random is predictable by design — never use it for passwords, tokens or anything security-related. Use secrets.

secrets, hashlib, uuid, base64
Python
import secrets, hashlib, hmac, uuid, base64

token = secrets.token_urlsafe(16)                  # unpredictable, URL-safe
print(len(token) >= 16)                            # True
print(secrets.compare_digest("abc", "abc"))        # True   ← constant-time comparison

print(hashlib.sha256(b"hello").hexdigest()[:16])   # 2cf24dba5fb0a30e
sig = hmac.new(b"secret-key", b"payload", hashlib.sha256).hexdigest()
print(len(sig))                                    # 64

u = uuid.uuid4()                                   # random unique id
print(u.version, len(str(u)))                      # 4 36

encoded = base64.b64encode(b"hi there")
print(encoded, base64.b64decode(encoded))          # b'aGkgdGhlcmU=' b'hi there'
Key point

Hash passwords with a slow, salted algorithm — hashlib.scrypt or pbkdf2_hmac (or the argon2/bcrypt packages) — never plain sha256.

Text helpers: string, textwrap, difflib, pprint
Python
import string, textwrap, difflib, pprint

print(string.ascii_lowercase[:5], string.digits, string.punctuation[:4])   # abcde 0123456789 !"#$
print(textwrap.shorten("The quick brown fox jumps over the lazy dog", width=25))   # The quick brown fox [...]
print(textwrap.wrap("one two three four five", width=9))   # ['one two', 'three', 'four five']
print(difflib.get_close_matches("pyhton", ["python", "java", "ruby"]))   # ['python']
print(round(difflib.SequenceMatcher(None, "kitten", "sitting").ratio(), 2))   # 0.62
print(pprint.pformat({"b": [1, 2], "a": {"x": 1}}))        # {'a': {'x': 1}, 'b': [1, 2]}   ← keys sorted
urllib.parse, ipaddress, html
Python
from urllib.parse import urlparse, parse_qs, urlencode, quote
import ipaddress, html

u = urlparse("https://shop.example.com:8443/search?q=red+shoes&page=2#top")
print(u.scheme, u.hostname, u.port, u.path)        # https shop.example.com 8443 /search
print(parse_qs(u.query))                           # {'q': ['red shoes'], 'page': ['2']}
print(urlencode({"q": "café & co", "n": 3}))       # q=caf%C3%A9+%26+co&n=3
print(quote("a b/c"))                              # a%20b/c

net = ipaddress.ip_network("10.0.0.0/30")
print([str(h) for h in net.hosts()])               # ['10.0.0.1', '10.0.0.2']
print(ipaddress.ip_address("192.168.1.5").is_private)   # True
print(html.escape("<b>Tom & Jerry</b>"))           # &lt;b&gt;Tom &amp; Jerry&lt;/b&gt;
Compression & archives in memory
Python
import gzip, io, zipfile

raw = b"python " * 1000
packed = gzip.compress(raw)
print(len(raw), len(packed) < 100, gzip.decompress(packed) == raw)   # 7000 True True

buf = io.BytesIO()
with zipfile.ZipFile(buf, "w", compression=zipfile.ZIP_DEFLATED) as zf:
    zf.writestr("notes/a.txt", "alpha")
    zf.writestr("notes/b.txt", "beta")
with zipfile.ZipFile(buf) as zf:
    print(zf.namelist(), zf.read("notes/b.txt"))   # ['notes/a.txt', 'notes/b.txt'] b'beta'
weakref, contextvars, dis
Python
import weakref, dis

class Big:
    pass

obj = Big()
ref = weakref.ref(obj)             # doesn't keep obj alive
print(ref() is obj)                # True
del obj
print(ref())                       # None   ← freed as soon as the last strong reference went

cache = weakref.WeakValueDictionary()   # entries vanish when values are garbage-collected

def add(a, b):
    return a + b
ops = [i.opname for i in dis.get_instructions(add)]
print("BINARY_OP" in ops)          # True   ← dis shows the bytecode CPython runs

contextvars

Per-task “global” state (request id, user) that works correctly with asyncio, unlike thread-locals.

copy

copy.copy / deepcopy / replace (3.13).

enum, dataclasses, typing

Model data clearly — see Object-Oriented Python.

sqlite3

A full SQL database in a file (or in memory), no server needed.

http.server

python -m http.server 8000 serves the current folder — handy for quick tests.

venv, pip, ensurepip

Environments and installers ship with Python.

Handy `python -m` one-liners
$ python -m http.server 8000        # static file server
$ python -m json.tool data.json     # pretty-print / validate JSON
$ python -m timeit "sum(range(1000))"
$ python -m venv .venv              # create a virtual environment
$ python -m zipfile -l bundle.zip   # list an archive
$ python -m calendar 2026 9         # print a month calendar
When should you reach for a third-party package instead of the standard library?

When the stdlib version is low-level or missing features you need: requests/httpx over urllib.request, pandas over csv for analysis, pydantic for validation, rich/click for CLIs, dateutil for fuzzy date parsing. Start with the stdlib — it has no install step and is stable.

random vs secrets?

random uses the Mersenne Twister — fast and reproducible with a seed, great for simulations and tests, but predictable. secrets uses the OS's cryptographically secure source — use it for tokens, passwords and keys.

Concurrency

Concurrency: Threads, Processes & asyncio

Three tools for doing many things at once. Which one to pick depends on whether your program waits on I/O or burns CPU — because of the GIL.

GIL (Global Interpreter Lock) — a mutex in CPython that lets only one thread execute Python bytecode at a time. Threads still help while waiting on I/O (the GIL is released), but they don't speed up CPU-bound Python code.

threadingmultiprocessingasyncio
ModelOS threads, shared memorySeparate processes, separate memoryOne thread, cooperative tasks
Best forI/O-bound (network, disk)CPU-bound (math, image processing)Many concurrent I/O waits (thousands of sockets)
GILLimits CPU parallelismBypassed — each process has its ownNot relevant — single thread
CostLight; needs locks for shared dataHeavy start-up; data must be pickledVery light; code must be async
Concurrency vs parallelism

Concurrency

Dealing with many things at once: tasks make progress in overlapping time periods, possibly by taking turns on one core. Threads under the GIL and asyncio give you this.

Parallelism

Doing many things at once: tasks literally run at the same instant on different cores. In CPython that means processes (or a free-threaded build, or C extensions that release the GIL).

The GIL in detail
  • What it protects: CPython's internals — above all the reference counts on every object. Without it, two threads doing refcount += 1 could corrupt memory.
  • When it is released: during blocking I/O (socket.recv, file.read, time.sleep), and inside many C extensions doing heavy work (NumPy, hashlib, zlib). The running thread is also forced to hand it over every few milliseconds (sys.getswitchinterval(), 5 ms by default).
  • What it does NOT do: make your code thread-safe. counter += 1 is several bytecodes, and a switch can happen between them — you still need locks.
  • Free-threaded build: CPython 3.13 added an optional build without the GIL (python3.13t); in 3.14 it is officially supported (PEP 779) as python3.14t. Pure-Python threads then run truly in parallel, at a small single-thread cost.
Python
import sys
print(sys.getswitchinterval())                 # 0.005
# Only exists on 3.13+; True on the normal build, False on a free-threaded one:
gil = getattr(sys, "_is_gil_enabled", lambda: True)()
print(isinstance(gil, bool))                   # True
Which tool? A decision flow
1
Is the work mostly waiting?
Network calls, disk, databases, sleeping → it's I/O-bound.
2
I/O-bound, a handful to hundreds of tasks
ThreadPoolExecutor — works with ordinary blocking libraries like requests.
3
I/O-bound, thousands of connections
asyncio with async libraries (aiohttp, httpx, asyncpg) — one thread, tiny per-task cost.
4
Is it computing in Python?
Loops, parsing, math → CPU-bound: ProcessPoolExecutor / multiprocessing, or vectorise with NumPy, or a free-threaded build.
5
Mixed?
asyncio for the I/O, loop.run_in_executor / asyncio.to_thread or a process pool for the heavy bits.
Threads for I/O
Python
from concurrent.futures import ThreadPoolExecutor
import time

def fetch(i):
    time.sleep(0.2)              # stands in for a network call (releases the GIL)
    return i * i

start = time.perf_counter()
with ThreadPoolExecutor(max_workers=5) as pool:
    results = list(pool.map(fetch, range(5)))
elapsed = time.perf_counter() - start
print(results, elapsed < 0.5)    # [0, 1, 4, 9, 16] True
Processes for CPU
Python
from concurrent.futures import ProcessPoolExecutor

def heavy(n):
    return sum(i * i for i in range(n))

if __name__ == "__main__":       # required: child processes re-import this module
    with ProcessPoolExecutor() as pool:
        print(list(pool.map(heavy, [10**6] * 4)))
asyncio for massive I/O concurrency
Python
import asyncio

async def download(name, delay):
    await asyncio.sleep(delay)   # yields control while "waiting"
    return f"{name} done"

async def main():
    results = await asyncio.gather(download("a", 0.1), download("b", 0.1))
    print(results)               # ['a done', 'b done']

asyncio.run(main())
Shared state needs a lock
Python
import threading

counter = 0
lock = threading.Lock()

def work():
    global counter
    for _ in range(100_000):
        with lock:               # without the lock, updates can be lost
            counter += 1

threads = [threading.Thread(target=work) for _ in range(4)]
for t in threads: t.start()
for t in threads: t.join()
print(counter)                   # 400000
Key point

counter += 1 is several bytecode steps (load, add, store), so a thread switch in between loses updates. Protect shared mutable state with a Lock or use a queue.Queue.

Recent change

Python 3.13 shipped an experimental free-threaded build (no GIL); 3.14 makes it officially supported but still optional — the default python3 keeps the GIL. For interviews, explain the GIL as above and mention free-threading as the direction of travel.

If Python has a GIL, why are threads useful at all?

Because most real programs spend their time waiting — for the network, the disk or a database. A thread blocked on I/O releases the GIL, so other threads run meanwhile. Threads give concurrency for I/O-bound work; for CPU-bound pure-Python work use processes (or a free-threaded build).

Does the GIL make my code thread-safe?

No. It only protects the interpreter's internals. A compound operation like counter += 1 or check-then-act (if key not in d: d[key] = …) can be interrupted between bytecodes, so shared mutable state still needs a Lock, a queue.Queue, or another synchronisation primitive.

Concurrency

Threading & Synchronisation

threading runs functions in OS threads that share one memory space. Sharing is the superpower and the danger — the synchronisation primitives (Lock, Event, Queue, …) are how you stay correct.

Thread — an independent flow of execution inside one process. All threads see the same globals and heap objects, so they can communicate cheaply — and corrupt each other's data if they write without coordination.

Creating, starting & joining threads

Pass a target callable (plus args/kwargs), or subclass Thread and override run(). start() launches the thread; join() waits for it to finish.

Python
import threading

results = {}

def square(n):
    results[n] = n * n                    # each thread writes its own key

threads = [threading.Thread(target=square, args=(i,), name=f"worker-{i}") for i in range(4)]
for t in threads:
    t.start()
for t in threads:
    t.join()                              # wait for all of them
print(sorted(results.items()))            # [(0, 0), (1, 1), (2, 4), (3, 9)]
print(threads[0].name, threads[0].is_alive())   # worker-0 False

class Downloader(threading.Thread):       # subclass style
    def __init__(self, url):
        super().__init__()
        self.url = url
        self.size = None
    def run(self):                        # called in the new thread by start()
        self.size = len(self.url)         # stands in for a real download

d = Downloader("https://example.com")
d.start(); d.join()
print(d.size)                             # 19
Watch out

Call start(), not run(). t.run() just executes the function in the current thread — no concurrency at all, and no error to tell you.

Method / attributeMeaning
t.start()Begin running run() in a new OS thread (once per thread object)
t.join(timeout=None)Block until the thread ends (or the timeout passes)
t.is_alive()Still running?
t.daemonDaemon threads don't keep the program alive
threading.current_thread()The Thread object running this code
threading.active_count()Number of live threads
Daemon threads

The interpreter exits when all non-daemon threads have finished. Daemon threads are killed abruptly at exit — fine for background heartbeats, dangerous for anything that writes files or commits transactions.

Python
import threading

def heartbeat(stop):
    while not stop.is_set():
        stop.wait(0.01)                   # sleep, but wake up immediately when stopped

stop = threading.Event()
t = threading.Thread(target=heartbeat, args=(stop,), daemon=True)
t.start()
print(t.daemon, t.is_alive())             # True True
stop.set(); t.join()                      # a clean shutdown is still better
print(t.is_alive())                       # False
Race conditions: why x += 1 isn't safe

A thread switch can happen between any two bytecodes. counter += 1 is a read, an add and a write — three steps:

Python
import dis

def increment():
    global counter
    counter += 1

ops = [i.opname for i in dis.get_instructions(increment)]
print([op for op in ops if op in ("LOAD_GLOBAL", "BINARY_OP", "STORE_GLOBAL")])
# ['LOAD_GLOBAL', 'BINARY_OP', 'STORE_GLOBAL']
StepThread AThread Bcounter
1reads 55
2reads 55
3computes 6, writes 66
4computes 6, writes 66 — one increment lost
Key point

Races are non-deterministic: the code may pass a thousand runs and fail in production. The fix is to make the read-modify-write atomic with a lock.

Lock & RLock
Python
import threading

class BankAccount:
    def __init__(self):
        self.balance = 0
        self._lock = threading.Lock()

    def deposit(self, amount):
        with self._lock:                  # acquire … release, even on exceptions
            self.balance += amount

acct = BankAccount()

def worker():
    for _ in range(10_000):
        acct.deposit(1)

threads = [threading.Thread(target=worker) for _ in range(5)]
for t in threads: t.start()
for t in threads: t.join()
print(acct.balance)                       # 50000

lock = threading.Lock()
lock.acquire()
print(lock.acquire(timeout=0.01))         # False  ← a Lock is not re-entrant
lock.release()

A plain Lock deadlocks if the same thread tries to take it twice. An RLock (re-entrant lock) counts acquisitions by its owner — handy when one locked method calls another.

Python
import threading

class Inventory:
    def __init__(self):
        self._lock = threading.RLock()
        self.items = {}

    def add(self, name, qty=1):
        with self._lock:
            self.items[name] = self.items.get(name, 0) + qty

    def add_many(self, names):
        with self._lock:                  # held here…
            for n in names:
                self.add(n)               # …and re-acquired inside add(): OK with RLock

inv = Inventory()
inv.add_many(["pen", "pen", "ink"])
print(inv.items)                          # {'pen': 2, 'ink': 1}
Semaphore, Event & Condition

Semaphore(n)

A lock that up to n threads can hold at once — limit concurrent DB connections or API calls. BoundedSemaphore also errors if released too often.

Event

A thread-safe boolean flag: set(), clear(), wait(). Great for “start now” and “please stop” signals.

Condition

A lock plus wait/notify: threads sleep until some predicate becomes true — the building block of queues.

Barrier(n)

n threads each call wait(); all are released together once the last one arrives.

Python
import threading, time

sem = threading.Semaphore(2)              # at most 2 inside at a time
active = peak = 0
guard = threading.Lock()

def task():
    global active, peak
    with sem:
        with guard:
            active += 1
            peak = max(peak, active)
        time.sleep(0.01)
        with guard:
            active -= 1

threads = [threading.Thread(target=task) for _ in range(6)]
for t in threads: t.start()
for t in threads: t.join()
print(peak <= 2)                          # True

ready = threading.Event()
log = []

def waiter():
    ready.wait()                          # blocks until set()
    log.append("go")

w = threading.Thread(target=waiter); w.start()
log.append("setting up")
ready.set(); w.join()
print(log)                                # ['setting up', 'go']

cond = threading.Condition()
items, got = [], []

def consumer():
    with cond:
        cond.wait_for(lambda: len(items) >= 3)   # sleeps; re-checks after each notify
        got.extend(items)

c = threading.Thread(target=consumer); c.start()
for i in range(3):
    with cond:
        items.append(i)
        cond.notify()
c.join()
print(got)                                # [0, 1, 2]
Producer–consumer with queue.Queue

queue.Queue is already thread-safe and does all the locking for you. It's the cleanest way for threads to hand work to each other — prefer it to sharing lists and locks.

Python
import queue, threading

q = queue.Queue(maxsize=5)                # put() blocks when full → back-pressure
results = []
STOP = object()                           # a unique sentinel

def consumer():
    while True:
        n = q.get()
        if n is STOP:
            q.task_done()
            break
        results.append(n * n)             # list.append is safe; fancier updates need a lock
        q.task_done()

workers = [threading.Thread(target=consumer) for _ in range(3)]
for w in workers: w.start()
for n in range(10):                       # the producer
    q.put(n)
for _ in workers:
    q.put(STOP)                           # one sentinel per consumer
q.join()                                  # wait until every item is task_done()
for w in workers: w.join()
print(sorted(results))                    # [0, 1, 4, 9, 16, 25, 36, 49, 64, 81]
Queue classOrder
queue.QueueFIFO
queue.LifoQueueLIFO (a stack)
queue.PriorityQueueSmallest item first — put (priority, item) tuples
queue.SimpleQueueUnbounded FIFO without task_done/join — simpler, faster
3.13+

Since 3.13, Queue.shutdown() wakes blocked get()/put() callers with queue.ShutDown — an alternative to sentinel values.

Per-thread data: threading.local
Python
import threading

ctx = threading.local()                   # each thread sees its own attributes
seen = {}

def current_user_greeting():
    return f"hi {ctx.user}"               # no need to pass user down the call chain

def handle(user):
    ctx.user = user
    seen[user] = current_user_greeting()

threads = [threading.Thread(target=handle, args=(u,)) for u in ("asha", "ravi")]
for t in threads: t.start()
for t in threads: t.join()
print(sorted(seen.values()))              # ['hi asha', 'hi ravi']
print(hasattr(ctx, "user"))               # False  ← the main thread never set it
Deadlocks & lock ordering

Thread 1 holds lock A and waits for B; thread 2 holds B and waits for A — both wait forever. The standard cure is to always acquire locks in one global order.

Python
import threading

class Account:
    def __init__(self, id, balance):
        self.id, self.balance = id, balance
        self.lock = threading.Lock()

def transfer(src, dst, amount):
    first, second = sorted((src, dst), key=lambda a: a.id)   # fixed order, whatever the direction
    with first.lock, second.lock:
        src.balance -= amount
        dst.balance += amount

a, b = Account(1, 100), Account(2, 100)
t1 = threading.Thread(target=lambda: [transfer(a, b, 1) for _ in range(1000)])
t2 = threading.Thread(target=lambda: [transfer(b, a, 1) for _ in range(1000)])
t1.start(); t2.start(); t1.join(); t2.join()
print(a.balance, b.balance)               # 100 100
  • Take locks in a consistent order (by id, by name, by address).
  • Hold locks for as short a time as possible; never call unknown code (callbacks, I/O) while holding one.
  • Use lock.acquire(timeout=…) to detect a stuck lock instead of hanging forever.
  • Prefer message passing (queue.Queue) over many fine-grained locks.
Timer
Python
import threading

fired = threading.Event()
t = threading.Timer(0.01, fired.set)      # run fired.set() after 10 ms, in a new thread
t.start()
print(fired.wait(timeout=1))              # True

never = threading.Timer(10, print, args=("never printed",))
never.start()
never.cancel(); never.join()              # cancel before it fires
print(never.is_alive())                   # False
Which built-in operations are thread-safe?
Usually atomic (single operation on a built-in)NOT atomic — needs a lock
L.append(x), L.pop(), L.extend(ys)x += 1, L[i] += 1, d[k] += 1
d[k] = v, d.get(k), d.pop(k, None)if k not in d: d[k] = v (check-then-act)
x = L[i], q.put(x), q.get()d[k] = d.get(k, 0) + 1
d.setdefault(k, v)Any sequence of several operations that must stay consistent
Watch out

Atomicity of single built-in operations is a CPython implementation detail (the GIL on the default build, per-object locks on the free-threaded build). It's fine to rely on queue.Queue; for your own invariants, use a lock.

What is a race condition and how do you prevent one?

The result depends on the unpredictable interleaving of threads — typically two threads doing read-modify-write on shared data. Prevent it by making the critical section atomic with a Lock, by confining data to one thread (queue.Queue, threading.local), or by using immutable data.

Lock vs RLock?

A Lock can be acquired once; a second acquire() — even from the same thread — blocks. An RLock records its owner and a counter, so the owning thread can re-acquire it (it must release it the same number of times). Use RLock when locked methods call other locked methods.

What is a deadlock and how do you avoid it?

Two or more threads each wait for a lock held by another, so none can proceed. Avoid it with a global lock-ordering rule, minimal lock scope, timeouts on acquire, and message passing instead of shared locks.

Concurrency

Multiprocessing

multiprocessing runs code in separate processes — each with its own interpreter, memory and GIL — so CPU-bound Python work finally uses every core.

Process — an independent program instance with its own address space. Processes share nothing by default; data moves between them by pickling it through pipes or queues.

Processes vs threads
ThreadsProcesses
MemorySharedSeparate (copy or explicit sharing)
GILOne GIL for all threadsOne GIL per process → true parallelism
Start-up cost~microseconds~milliseconds (a new interpreter)
CommunicationAny object, directlyPickled messages: Queue, Pipe, shared memory
A crashCan take down the whole programIsolated to that process
Use forI/O-bound workCPU-bound work
Process: start, join, exit code
Python
from multiprocessing import Process, current_process

def work(n):
    print(f"{current_process().name}: {n * n}")

if __name__ == "__main__":
    procs = [Process(target=work, args=(i,), name=f"P{i}") for i in range(3)]
    for p in procs:
        p.start()
    for p in procs:
        p.join()
    print("exit codes:", [p.exitcode for p in procs])
Output (the P lines can appear in any order)
P0: 0
P1: 1
P2: 4
exit codes: [0, 0, 0]
Required

The if __name__ == "__main__": guard is mandatory with the spawn and forkserver start methods: each child re-imports your module, and without the guard it would start children of its own, recursively.

Pool: parallel map
Python
from multiprocessing import Pool

def square(x):
    return x * x

def power(base, exp):
    return base ** exp

if __name__ == "__main__":
    with Pool(processes=4) as pool:
        print(pool.map(square, range(6)))                 # [0, 1, 4, 9, 16, 25]
        print(list(pool.imap(square, range(3))))          # [0, 1, 4]  ← lazy, in order
        print(sorted(pool.imap_unordered(square, range(3))))   # [0, 1, 4]  ← as they finish
        print(pool.starmap(power, [(2, 3), (3, 2)]))      # [8, 9]  ← unpacks tuples
        r = pool.apply_async(square, (7,))
        print(r.get(timeout=5))                           # 49
MethodReturnsUse when
map(f, it, chunksize)A list, in input order (blocks)All inputs known, results fit in memory
imap(f, it)A lazy iterator, in orderLarge inputs; process results as a stream
imap_unordered(f, it)A lazy iterator, completion orderOrder doesn't matter — fastest feedback
starmap(f, pairs)A list; each tuple is unpacked into argsFunctions of several arguments
apply_async(f, args)An AsyncResult (.get())One-off background calls
Performance

For many tiny tasks, pass a larger chunksize to map/imap — sending each item separately costs more in pickling and IPC than the work itself.

Start methods
MethodHow the child startsDefault on
spawnA fresh interpreter imports your module, then runs the target. Safe, slower.Windows, macOS (since 3.8)
forkThe OS copies the parent process (memory included). Fast, but unsafe if the parent has threads.Linux before 3.14
forkserverA clean server process is started once and forks children on request.Linux since 3.14
Python
import multiprocessing as mp

print(mp.cpu_count() > 0)                                     # True
print(mp.get_start_method() in {"fork", "spawn", "forkserver"})   # True
print("spawn" in mp.get_all_start_methods())                  # True  ← available everywhere

Choose explicitly with mp.set_start_method("spawn") once at start-up, or better, a context: ctx = mp.get_context("spawn"); ctx.Pool(...). Code that works under spawn works everywhere.

Everything crosses the boundary by pickling

Arguments, return values and queue items are pickled in the parent and unpickled in the child. Functions are pickled by name, so the target must be importable: a module-level def, not a lambda, nested function or something defined in the REPL.

Python
import pickle

def module_level(x):
    return x

try:
    pickle.dumps(lambda x: x)
except pickle.PicklingError:
    print("lambdas can't be sent to a worker")   # lambdas can't be sent to a worker
Watch out

Large arguments are copied for every task. Send small descriptions (file paths, index ranges) and let workers load the data, or use shared memory.

Communicating: Queue & Pipe
Python
from multiprocessing import Process, Queue, Pipe

def producer(q):
    for i in range(3):
        q.put(i * 10)
    q.put(None)                           # sentinel: "no more"

def echo(conn):
    msg = conn.recv()
    conn.send(msg.upper())
    conn.close()

if __name__ == "__main__":
    q = Queue()
    p = Process(target=producer, args=(q,)); p.start()
    items = []
    while (item := q.get()) is not None:
        items.append(item)
    p.join()
    print(items)                          # [0, 10, 20]

    parent, child = Pipe()                # two connected ends
    p = Process(target=echo, args=(child,)); p.start()
    parent.send("hello")
    print(parent.recv())                  # HELLO
    p.join()
Sharing state

Value / Array

A single number or fixed array in shared memory, with an optional lock: Value('i', 0), Array('d', 10).

shared_memory

A raw named block of bytes any process can attach to — zero-copy for NumPy arrays (3.8+).

Manager()

A server process holding real dict/list objects; others use proxies. Flexible but slow (every access is IPC).

Python
from multiprocessing import Process, Value, Manager

def add(counter, n):
    for _ in range(n):
        with counter.get_lock():          # += on shared memory still needs the lock
            counter.value += 1

def record(d, key):
    d[key] = key * 2

if __name__ == "__main__":
    counter = Value("i", 0)
    ps = [Process(target=add, args=(counter, 1000)) for _ in range(4)]
    for p in ps: p.start()
    for p in ps: p.join()
    print(counter.value)                  # 4000

    with Manager() as m:
        d = m.dict()
        ps = [Process(target=record, args=(d, k)) for k in range(3)]
        for p in ps: p.start()
        for p in ps: p.join()
        print(dict(sorted(d.items())))    # {0: 0, 1: 2, 2: 4}

shared_memory hands out a named buffer; another process attaches by name. Here both ends live in one process to show the API:

Python
from multiprocessing import shared_memory

shm = shared_memory.SharedMemory(create=True, size=4)
try:
    shm.buf[:4] = bytes([1, 2, 3, 4])
    other = shared_memory.SharedMemory(name=shm.name)   # normally done in another process
    print(list(other.buf[:4]))                          # [1, 2, 3, 4]
    other.close()
finally:
    shm.close()
    shm.unlink()                          # free the block (only the creator does this)
Do

Use processes for CPU-heavy, independent chunks of work; keep messages small; prefer ProcessPoolExecutor or Pool over hand-managed Process objects.

Avoid

Spawning a process per tiny task, sharing huge objects through a Manager, or forgetting the __main__ guard.

Why does multiprocessing need if __name__ == "__main__"?

With spawn/forkserver the child process imports your main module to find the target function. Without the guard, that import would execute the process-creating code again — infinite recursion (Python raises a RuntimeError to stop it).

How do processes share data?

By default they don't — each has its own memory. You send pickled messages (Queue, Pipe, pool arguments/results), or share explicitly with Value/Array, shared_memory, or a Manager proxy.

fork vs spawn?

fork copies the running parent (fast, inherits everything — including held locks from other threads, which can deadlock). spawn starts a fresh interpreter (slower, but clean and portable). macOS and Windows default to spawn; Linux moved from fork to forkserver in 3.14.

Concurrency

concurrent.futures

One high-level API for both threads and processes: submit callables to an executor and get back futures — placeholders for results that will arrive later.

Future — an object representing a pending result. You can ask whether it's done(), block on result(), fetch its exception(), cancel() it, or attach callbacks.

The Executor API
CallDoes
ex.submit(f, *args, **kw)Schedule one call → returns a Future immediately
ex.map(f, *iterables, timeout=None)Like built-in map, run in parallel; results in input order
ex.shutdown(wait=True, cancel_futures=False)Stop accepting work; called automatically by with
as_completed(futures, timeout)Yield futures as they finish
wait(futures, timeout, return_when)Block until ALL_COMPLETED / FIRST_COMPLETED / FIRST_EXCEPTION
submit, result & map
Python
from concurrent.futures import ThreadPoolExecutor

def square(x):
    return x * x

with ThreadPoolExecutor(max_workers=3) as ex:
    fut = ex.submit(square, 7)
    print(fut.result())                   # 49  ← blocks until ready
    print(fut.done())                     # True
    print(list(ex.map(square, [3, 1, 2])))   # [9, 1, 4]  ← input order, always
as_completed: handle results as they arrive
Python
import time
from concurrent.futures import ThreadPoolExecutor, as_completed

def job(name, delay):
    time.sleep(delay)                     # stands in for a network call
    return name

with ThreadPoolExecutor() as ex:
    futures = {ex.submit(job, n, d): n for n, d in [("slow", 0.05), ("fast", 0.0), ("mid", 0.02)]}
    finished = [f.result() for f in as_completed(futures)]   # completion order
print(sorted(finished))                   # ['fast', 'mid', 'slow']
print(len(futures))                       # 3
Key point

Map each future back to its input with a dict ({future: input}), because as_completed yields them in whatever order they finish.

Exceptions travel through the future

An exception raised in a worker is stored in the future and re-raised when you call result() — nothing is printed if you never ask.

Python
from concurrent.futures import ThreadPoolExecutor

def risky(x):
    if x == 2:
        raise ValueError("bad input 2")
    return x

with ThreadPoolExecutor() as ex:
    futures = [ex.submit(risky, i) for i in range(4)]
    outcomes = []
    for f in futures:
        try:
            outcomes.append(f.result())
        except ValueError as e:
            outcomes.append(f"error: {e}")
print(outcomes)                           # [0, 1, 'error: bad input 2', 3]
print(type(futures[2].exception()).__name__)   # ValueError
Watch out

With ex.map, the first failing item raises when the iterator reaches it, and you lose the remaining results. Use submit + per-future try when some inputs may fail.

wait, cancel & timeouts
Python
import threading
from concurrent.futures import ThreadPoolExecutor, wait, FIRST_COMPLETED

gate = threading.Event()
with ThreadPoolExecutor(max_workers=2) as ex:
    quick = ex.submit(lambda: "quick")
    blocked = ex.submit(gate.wait)        # can't finish until the gate opens
    done, pending = wait([quick, blocked], return_when=FIRST_COMPLETED)
    print([f.result() for f in done], len(pending))   # ['quick'] 1
    gate.set()
Python
import threading
from concurrent.futures import ThreadPoolExecutor

started, gate = threading.Event(), threading.Event()

def blocker():
    started.set()
    gate.wait()
    return "finished"

with ThreadPoolExecutor(max_workers=1) as ex:
    running = ex.submit(blocker)          # occupies the only worker
    queued = ex.submit(print, "never")    # waits in the queue
    started.wait()
    print(queued.cancel(), queued.cancelled())   # True True  ← not started yet
    print(running.cancel())               # False  ← already running: can't cancel
    try:
        running.result(timeout=0.01)
    except TimeoutError:
        print("timed out")                # timed out
    gate.set()
print(running.result())                   # finished
Key point

A timeout on result() only stops you waiting — the task keeps running. Threads can't be killed; design long tasks to check a stop Event.

Callbacks
Python
from concurrent.futures import ThreadPoolExecutor

log = []
with ThreadPoolExecutor() as ex:
    f = ex.submit(sum, [1, 2, 3])
    f.add_done_callback(lambda fut: log.append(fut.result() * 10))
print(log)                                # [60]
Processes & interpreters: same API
Python
from concurrent.futures import ProcessPoolExecutor

def count_primes(limit):
    return sum(all(n % d for d in range(2, int(n ** 0.5) + 1)) for n in range(2, limit))

if __name__ == "__main__":
    with ProcessPoolExecutor() as ex:     # one worker per CPU core by default
        print(list(ex.map(count_primes, [10_000, 20_000], chunksize=1)))   # [1229, 2262]
ExecutorParallel CPU?Default max_workersNotes
ThreadPoolExecutorNo (GIL)min(32, cpu_count + 4)I/O-bound; shares memory
ProcessPoolExecutorYesnumber of CPUsArguments/results must pickle; needs __main__ guard
InterpreterPoolExecutor (3.14)Yesnumber of CPUsSub-interpreters in one process, each with its own GIL
Do

Size thread pools for the I/O you're waiting on (tens of workers is fine); size process pools to the number of cores.

Avoid

Using a ProcessPoolExecutor for tiny tasks — pickling and IPC overhead will make it slower than a plain loop.

What's the difference between ex.map and submit + as_completed?

map returns results in input order and raises the first exception when you reach it — simple and tidy. submit returns one Future per call; with as_completed you process each result as soon as it's ready and handle failures individually.

How do you get an exception out of a worker thread?

Call future.result() — it re-raises the worker's exception in the caller — or inspect future.exception(). With raw threading.Thread, exceptions are only reported by threading.excepthook, which is why executors are usually nicer.

Concurrency

asyncio in Depth

asyncio runs thousands of I/O tasks on one thread by switching between them at every await. It's cooperative multitasking: fast and lock-free, as long as nobody blocks the loop.

Event loop — a scheduler that runs ready tasks one at a time. A task runs until it awaits something that isn't ready (a socket, a timer); the loop then parks it and runs another task, resuming it when its I/O completes.

Coroutines, tasks & futures
ObjectWhat it isCreated by
CoroutineThe paused computation returned by calling an async def function. Does nothing until awaited or wrapped in a task.coro = fetch()
TaskA coroutine scheduled on the loop — it runs concurrently with the code that created it.asyncio.create_task(coro), TaskGroup.create_task
FutureA low-level placeholder for a result; tasks are futures. Rarely created by hand.loop.create_future()
Python
import asyncio

async def greet(name):
    await asyncio.sleep(0)                # yield control to the loop once
    return f"hi {name}"

coro = greet("asha")
print(type(coro).__name__)                # coroutine  ← nothing has run yet
print(asyncio.run(coro))                  # hi asha    ← run() starts a loop, runs it, closes it
Sequential awaits vs concurrent tasks
Python
import asyncio

async def job(name, delay, log):
    log.append(f"start {name}")
    await asyncio.sleep(delay)
    log.append(f"end {name}")

async def sequential():
    log = []
    await job("a", 0.02, log)             # waits for a to finish…
    await job("b", 0.01, log)             # …before b even starts
    return log

async def concurrent():
    log = []
    t1 = asyncio.create_task(job("a", 0.02, log))   # both scheduled now
    t2 = asyncio.create_task(job("b", 0.01, log))
    await t1
    await t2
    return log

print(asyncio.run(sequential()))   # ['start a', 'end a', 'start b', 'end b']
print(asyncio.run(concurrent()))   # ['start a', 'start b', 'end b', 'end a']
Watch out

await coro() one after another is sequential. Concurrency only happens when several tasks exist at once — via create_task, gather or a TaskGroup.

gather
Python
import asyncio

async def fetch(i):
    await asyncio.sleep(0.01 * (3 - i))   # later items finish first…
    if i == 1:
        raise ValueError(f"item {i} failed")
    return i * 10

async def main():
    results = await asyncio.gather(*(fetch(i) for i in range(3)), return_exceptions=True)
    print(results)                        # [0, ValueError('item 1 failed'), 20]  ← …but order = input order
    try:
        await asyncio.gather(fetch(0), fetch(1))
    except ValueError as e:
        print("first error:", e)          # first error: item 1 failed

asyncio.run(main())
TaskGroup — structured concurrency (3.11+)

async with asyncio.TaskGroup() waits for every task created in it. If one fails, the others are cancelled and all errors are raised together as an ExceptionGroup — no orphaned tasks.

Python
import asyncio

async def worker(n):
    await asyncio.sleep(0.01)
    if n == 2:
        raise KeyError(n)
    return n

async def main():
    async with asyncio.TaskGroup() as tg:
        tasks = [tg.create_task(worker(n)) for n in range(2)]
    print([t.result() for t in tasks])    # [0, 1]  ← all done when the block exits

    try:
        async with asyncio.TaskGroup() as tg:
            tg.create_task(worker(2))
            slow = tg.create_task(asyncio.sleep(10))
    except* KeyError as eg:
        print(type(eg).__name__, len(eg.exceptions))   # ExceptionGroup 1
    print(slow.cancelled())               # True  ← the sibling was cancelled

asyncio.run(main())
Key point

Prefer TaskGroup over bare create_task + gather in new code. A bare task that nobody keeps a reference to can even be garbage-collected mid-flight — keep references or use a group.

Timeouts & cancellation
Python
import asyncio

async def main():
    try:
        async with asyncio.timeout(0.01):          # 3.11+
            await asyncio.sleep(1)
    except TimeoutError:
        print("timeout block")                     # timeout block

    try:
        await asyncio.wait_for(asyncio.sleep(1), timeout=0.01)
    except TimeoutError:
        print("wait_for")                          # wait_for

asyncio.run(main())
Python
import asyncio

async def worker(log):
    try:
        await asyncio.sleep(1)
    except asyncio.CancelledError:
        log.append("cleaning up")
        raise                             # always re-raise CancelledError

async def main():
    log = []
    t = asyncio.create_task(worker(log))
    await asyncio.sleep(0)                # let it start
    t.cancel()                            # throws CancelledError into it at its await
    try:
        await t
    except asyncio.CancelledError:
        log.append("cancelled")
    print(log, t.cancelled())             # ['cleaning up', 'cancelled'] True

asyncio.run(main())
Watch out

Swallowing CancelledError (except Exception doesn't catch it, but a bare except: or except BaseException does) breaks timeouts and TaskGroup shutdown. Clean up, then re-raise.

Queues & semaphores
Python
import asyncio

async def consumer(q, out):
    while True:
        item = await q.get()
        out.append(item * item)
        q.task_done()

async def main():
    q = asyncio.Queue(maxsize=2)          # put() waits when full
    out = []
    workers = [asyncio.create_task(consumer(q, out)) for _ in range(2)]
    for i in range(5):                    # the producer
        await q.put(i)
    await q.join()                        # every item processed
    for w in workers:
        w.cancel()
    await asyncio.gather(*workers, return_exceptions=True)
    print(sorted(out))                    # [0, 1, 4, 9, 16]

asyncio.run(main())
Python
import asyncio

async def main():
    sem = asyncio.Semaphore(3)            # at most 3 "requests" in flight
    active = peak = 0

    async def fetch(i):
        nonlocal active, peak
        async with sem:
            active += 1
            peak = max(peak, active)
            await asyncio.sleep(0.01)
            active -= 1
            return i

    results = await asyncio.gather(*(fetch(i) for i in range(10)))
    print(results == list(range(10)), peak)   # True 3

asyncio.run(main())
Key point

No lock is needed around active += 1 here: with one thread, a task can only be interrupted at an await.

Blocking code: asyncio.to_thread

A blocking call (time.sleep, requests.get, heavy CPU) freezes the whole loop — every other task stops. Push it to a worker thread:

Python
import asyncio, time

def blocking_io(x):
    time.sleep(0.01)                      # a blocking library call
    return x * 2

async def main():
    results = await asyncio.gather(
        asyncio.to_thread(blocking_io, 1),   # runs in the default thread pool
        asyncio.to_thread(blocking_io, 2),
    )
    print(results)                        # [2, 4]

asyncio.run(main())
Async iterators & context managers
Python
import asyncio
from contextlib import asynccontextmanager

async def ticker(n):                      # async generator
    for i in range(n):
        await asyncio.sleep(0)
        yield i

log = []

@asynccontextmanager
async def connection(name):               # async with → __aenter__ / __aexit__
    log.append(f"open {name}")
    try:
        yield name.upper()
    finally:
        log.append(f"close {name}")

async def main():
    squares = [i * i async for i in ticker(4)]   # async comprehension
    print(squares)                        # [0, 1, 4, 9]
    async with connection("db") as conn:
        log.append(f"using {conn}")
    print(log)                            # ['open db', 'using DB', 'close db']

asyncio.run(main())
Common mistakes
Python
import asyncio

async def save():
    return "saved"

async def main():
    result = save()                       # forgot await → just a coroutine object
    print(type(result).__name__)          # coroutine
    result.close()                        # (silences "coroutine was never awaited")
    print(await save())                   # saved

asyncio.run(main())
MistakeSymptomFix
Forgetting awaitRuntimeWarning: coroutine … was never awaited; nothing happensawait it or create a task
time.sleep() / requests in a coroutineAll tasks freezeawait asyncio.sleep(), async libraries, or asyncio.to_thread
CPU-heavy loop in a coroutineLatency spikes for every other taskloop.run_in_executor(ProcessPoolExecutor(), …)
Calling asyncio.run inside a running loopRuntimeErrorawait the coroutine instead
Fire-and-forget tasks without a referenceTask silently disappears; errors never seenKeep references / use TaskGroup
Sync vs async at a glance
SyncAsync
def f():async def f():
f()await f()
time.sleep(1)await asyncio.sleep(1)
for x in it:async for x in ait:
with cm:async with cm:
threading.Lockasyncio.Lock (only across awaits)
queue.Queueasyncio.Queue
ThreadPoolExecutor.mapasyncio.gather / TaskGroup
How is asyncio different from threading?

asyncio uses one thread and cooperative switching: tasks yield only at await, so there are no surprise interleavings and tasks are very cheap (thousands are fine). Threads are pre-emptive: the OS can switch at any bytecode, so shared data needs locks, and each thread costs more memory. Neither gives CPU parallelism under the GIL.

What happens if you call a blocking function inside a coroutine?

It blocks the event loop thread, so every other task stalls until it returns. Use the async equivalent, or offload with await asyncio.to_thread(func, …) (or run_in_executor with a process pool for CPU work).

gather vs TaskGroup?

Both run awaitables concurrently. gather returns results in order and, by default, propagates the first exception while leaving the other tasks running (or collects errors with return_exceptions=True). TaskGroup (3.11+) guarantees structure: if any task fails, the rest are cancelled and all failures are raised as an ExceptionGroup.

Testing & Tooling

Testing: unittest & pytest

Automated tests prove your code works today and keep it working tomorrow. Python ships unittest and doctest; most projects use pytest for its plain asserts and fixtures.

Why and what to test

Unit tests

One function or class in isolation. Fast (milliseconds) — the bulk of your suite.

Integration tests

Several parts together: your code + a real database, file system or HTTP layer.

End-to-end tests

The whole system as a user sees it. Slow and brittle — keep few of them.

Regression tests

Every fixed bug gets a test that failed before the fix, so it can't come back.

A good test follows Arrange → Act → Assert: set up inputs, call the code once, check the result.

assert — the building block
Python
def slugify(title: str) -> str:
    return "-".join(title.lower().split())

# Arrange, Act, Assert — a test is just code that raises if something is wrong
assert slugify("Hello World") == "hello-world"
assert slugify("  Python   Notes ") == "python-notes"
try:
    assert slugify("A B") == "a_b", "separator should be a hyphen"
except AssertionError as e:
    print("failed:", e)             # failed: separator should be a hyphen
Watch out

assert statements are removed when Python runs with -O. Use them in tests and for internal sanity checks — never to validate user input in production code.

unittest — the built-in framework
Python
import io, unittest

class Stack:
    def __init__(self):
        self._items = []
    def push(self, x):
        self._items.append(x)
    def pop(self):
        if not self._items:
            raise IndexError("pop from empty stack")
        return self._items.pop()

class StackTest(unittest.TestCase):
    def setUp(self):                         # runs before EACH test method
        self.s = Stack()

    def test_push_then_pop(self):
        self.s.push(1)
        self.s.push(2)
        self.assertEqual(self.s.pop(), 2)

    def test_pop_empty_raises(self):
        with self.assertRaises(IndexError):
            self.s.pop()

    def test_many_values(self):
        for value in [0, "x", None]:
            with self.subTest(value=value):  # each case reported separately
                self.s.push(value)
                self.assertIs(self.s.pop(), value)

suite = unittest.defaultTestLoader.loadTestsFromTestCase(StackTest)
result = unittest.TextTestRunner(stream=io.StringIO(), verbosity=0).run(suite)
print(result.testsRun, len(result.failures), len(result.errors))   # 3 0 0
print(result.wasSuccessful())                                        # True
AssertionChecks
assertEqual(a, b) / assertNotEquala == b
assertTrue(x) / assertFalse(x)Truthiness
assertIs(a, b) / assertIsNone(x)Identity
assertIn(a, b)Membership
assertRaises(E)The block raises E (use as a context manager)
assertAlmostEqual(a, b, places=7)Floats close enough
assertCountEqual(a, b)Same items, any order
Key point

setUp/tearDown run around every test; setUpClass/tearDownClass once per class. Test methods must start with test. Run everything with python -m unittest discover.

pytest — less boilerplate
Python
# test_cart.py
import pytest
from shop.cart import Cart

@pytest.fixture
def cart():                                   # fresh object for every test that asks for it
    c = Cart()
    c.add("pen", price=10, qty=2)
    return c

def test_total(cart):
    assert cart.total() == 20                 # plain assert — pytest shows both sides on failure

@pytest.mark.parametrize("qty, expected", [(1, 10), (3, 30), (0, 0)])
def test_line_total(qty, expected):
    c = Cart()
    c.add("pen", price=10, qty=qty)
    assert c.total() == expected

def test_negative_qty_rejected():
    with pytest.raises(ValueError, match="qty"):
        Cart().add("pen", price=10, qty=-1)

def test_export(cart, tmp_path):              # built-in fixture: a fresh temp directory
    path = tmp_path / "cart.json"
    cart.save(path)
    assert path.exists()
Terminal
$ pip install pytest
$ pytest                       # finds test_*.py files and test_* functions
$ pytest -q -k "total"         # quiet, only tests whose name matches
$ pytest -x --lf               # stop at first failure; rerun last failures
$ pytest --cov=shop            # coverage report (pytest-cov plugin)

doctest — examples that are tests
Python
import doctest, io

def median(xs):
    """Return the median of a non-empty list.

    >>> median([3, 1, 2])
    2
    >>> median([4, 1, 3, 2])
    2.5
    """
    s = sorted(xs)
    mid = len(s) // 2
    return s[mid] if len(s) % 2 else (s[mid - 1] + s[mid]) / 2

runner = doctest.DocTestRunner()
for test in doctest.DocTestFinder().find(median, globs={"median": median}):
    runner.run(test, out=io.StringIO().write)
print(runner.tries, runner.failures)       # 2 0
Terminal
$ python -m doctest -v stats.py     # run every docstring example in a module
$ pytest --doctest-modules          # or let pytest collect them
Coverage & the TDD loop
1
Red
Write a small failing test for the next bit of behaviour.
2
Green
Write the simplest code that makes it pass.
3
Refactor
Clean up with the tests as a safety net; rerun them.
Key point

Coverage (coverage run -m pytest && coverage report) shows which lines never ran. High coverage doesn't prove correctness, but uncovered code is definitely untested.

Do

Keep tests fast, independent and deterministic; one behaviour per test; descriptive names like test_pop_empty_raises.

Avoid

Tests that depend on each other's order, the current time, the network or random data without a fixed seed.

What is a fixture in pytest?

A function decorated with @pytest.fixture that prepares something a test needs (an object, a temp directory, a DB connection). Tests receive it by naming it as a parameter. Fixtures can yield to run teardown and have scopes (function, module, session).

unittest or pytest?

pytest for most projects: less boilerplate, better failure output, fixtures and a large plugin ecosystem — and it runs existing unittest tests. unittest when you can't add dependencies.

How do you test code that raises an exception?

with pytest.raises(ValueError, match="..."): or with self.assertRaises(ValueError): around the call. The test fails if the exception is not raised.

Testing & Tooling

Mocking & Test Doubles

A test double stands in for a slow, flaky or external dependency — an API, the clock, the database — so a unit test checks only your code. unittest.mock is the standard toolkit.

Kinds of test doubles
DoubleWhat it doesExample
StubReturns canned answersget_rate() always returns 83.0
MockRecords calls so you can assert on them“send_email was called once with this address”
FakeA working lightweight implementationAn in-memory dict instead of a real database
SpyWraps the real object and records callsMock(wraps=real_func)
Mock basics
Python
from unittest.mock import Mock

api = Mock()
api.get_user.return_value = {"id": 7, "name": "Asha"}   # stub a method

print(api.get_user(7)["name"])                    # Asha
print(api.get_user.call_count)                    # 1
print(api.get_user.call_args)                     # call(7)
api.get_user.assert_called_once_with(7)           # passes silently

print(type(api.anything.you.like).__name__)       # Mock   ← attributes are created on demand
side_effect: exceptions and sequences
Python
from unittest.mock import Mock

def fetch_with_retry(client, attempts=3):
    for i in range(attempts):
        try:
            return client.fetch()
        except ConnectionError:
            if i == attempts - 1:
                raise

client = Mock()
client.fetch.side_effect = [ConnectionError(), ConnectionError(), "data"]   # fail, fail, succeed
print(fetch_with_retry(client), client.fetch.call_count)   # data 3

client.fetch.side_effect = ConnectionError("down")          # always fail
try:
    fetch_with_retry(client)
except ConnectionError as e:
    print("gave up:", e)                                     # gave up: down

double = Mock(side_effect=lambda x: x * 2)                   # compute the return value
print(double(21))                                            # 42
patch: replace a name during a test

patch swaps an attribute for a mock and restores it afterwards. Patch where the name is looked up, not where it was defined: if app.py does from time import time, patch app.time, not time.time.

Python
import time
from unittest.mock import patch

class Greeter:
    def greeting(self):
        hour = time.localtime().tm_hour
        return "Good morning" if hour < 12 else "Good afternoon"

fake = time.struct_time((2026, 9, 29, 9, 0, 0, 1, 272, 0))
with patch.object(time, "localtime", return_value=fake):      # context-manager form
    print(Greeter().greeting())                                # Good morning

@patch.object(Greeter, "greeting", return_value="Hi")          # decorator form
def check(mock_greeting):
    result = Greeter().greeting()
    mock_greeting.assert_called_once()
    return result

print(check())                                                 # Hi
print(time.localtime is not None)                              # True   ← original restored
Python
import os
from unittest.mock import patch, mock_open

with patch.dict(os.environ, {"FEATURE_X": "on"}):     # temporary environment
    print(os.environ["FEATURE_X"])                    # on
print("FEATURE_X" in os.environ)                      # False

with patch("builtins.open", mock_open(read_data="line1\nline2")):
    with open("any/path.txt") as f:                   # no real file touched
        print(f.read().splitlines())                  # ['line1', 'line2']
spec & autospec: mocks that fail like the real thing
Python
from unittest.mock import create_autospec

class PaymentGateway:
    def charge(self, amount: int, currency: str) -> str:
        raise NotImplementedError

gateway = create_autospec(PaymentGateway, instance=True)
gateway.charge.return_value = "txn-1"
print(gateway.charge(500, "INR"))                     # txn-1

try:
    gateway.charge(500)                               # wrong signature → error, like the real method
except TypeError:
    print("signature checked")                        # signature checked
try:
    gateway.refund(1)                                 # no such method on the real class
except AttributeError:
    print("unknown method rejected")                  # unknown method rejected
Watch out

A plain Mock() accepts any call and any attribute, so a typo like mock.assert_caled_once() or a renamed method can make a broken test pass. Use spec=/autospec (and Python rejects assert_* typos since 3.12).

pytest's monkeypatch
Python
def test_uses_api_key(monkeypatch):
    monkeypatch.setenv("API_KEY", "test-key")
    monkeypatch.setattr("shop.payments.charge", lambda amount: "ok")
    assert checkout(100) == "ok"          # undone automatically after the test
Do

Mock at the boundaries: network, time, randomness, file system, third-party services.

Avoid

Mocking your own internal functions everywhere — tests then mirror the implementation and break on every refactor while missing real bugs.

Why do you patch where an object is used, not where it's defined?

from module import name copies the reference into the importing module's namespace. Patching the original module doesn't change that copy, so the code under test still calls the real function. Patch consumer_module.name.

Mock vs MagicMock?

MagicMock is a Mock that also pre-configures magic methods (__len__, __iter__, __enter__, …), so it can stand in for containers or context managers. patch uses MagicMock by default.

What is side_effect used for?

To make a mock raise an exception, return successive values from an iterable, or compute its result with a function — for testing error handling and retries.

Testing & Tooling

Debugging & Profiling

Debugging finds why code is wrong; profiling finds where it is slow or hungry. Both start with reading carefully and measuring, not guessing.

Reading a traceback
Traceback
Traceback (most recent call last):
  File "report.py", line 12, in <module>
    print(average(scores))
          ~~~~~~~^^^^^^^^
  File "report.py", line 4, in average
    return sum(xs) / len(xs)
           ~~~~~~~~^~~~~~~~~
ZeroDivisionError: division by zero
1
Read the last line first
The exception type and message: what went wrong.
2
Walk up the stack
Frames are listed oldest → newest; the last frame is where it was raised.
3
Find your code
Skip library frames; the lowest frame in your file is usually the place to fix.
4
Use the markers
Since 3.11 ~~~^^^ points at the exact sub-expression that failed.
print, logging and assertions
Python
items = [3, 5]
total = sum(items)
print(f"{items=} {total=}")          # items=[3, 5] total=8   ← the "=" specifier labels values
print(f"{total / len(items)=:.2f}")  # total / len(items)=4.00
pdb — the built-in debugger

Put breakpoint() where you want to stop (3.7+). The program pauses there with an interactive (Pdb) prompt; you can inspect variables, step line by line and continue.

Python
def apply_discount(price, pct):
    breakpoint()              # execution pauses here
    return price - price * pct / 100

Terminal
$ python -m pdb script.py                 # start under the debugger
$ python -m pdb -c continue script.py     # run; drop into pdb post-mortem on a crash
$ PYTHONBREAKPOINT=0 python script.py     # disable every breakpoint() call
Key point

IDE debuggers (VS Code, PyCharm) do the same with clicks: breakpoints, conditional breakpoints, watch expressions. faulthandler (python -X faulthandler) dumps tracebacks even on hard crashes and hangs.

timeit — micro-benchmarks
Python
import timeit

setup = "data = list(range(10_000)); s = set(data)"
list_time = timeit.timeit("9_999 in data", setup=setup, number=1_000)
set_time = timeit.timeit("9_999 in s", setup=setup, number=1_000)
print(set_time < list_time)          # True   ← O(1) hash lookup beats an O(n) scan
Terminal
$ python -m timeit -s "xs = list(range(1000))" "sum(xs)"

cProfile — where does the time go?
Python
import cProfile, pstats, io

def slow_square(n):
    return sum(i * i for i in range(n))

def work():
    return [slow_square(20_000) for _ in range(20)]

profiler = cProfile.Profile()
profiler.enable()
work()
profiler.disable()

out = io.StringIO()
stats = pstats.Stats(profiler, stream=out).sort_stats("cumulative")
stats.print_stats(5)
print("slow_square" in out.getvalue())     # True

Python
import tracemalloc

ToolKindGood for
cProfileDeterministic, built inWhich functions take the time

line_profilerLine by lineThe slow line inside a known-slow function
scaleneCPU + memory + GPU samplingAn all-in-one view

How would you find why a Python program is slow?

Reproduce with a realistic input, profile with cProfile (or py-spy on a live process), sort by cumulative time, then zoom in with line_profiler or timeit. Fix the biggest hotspot — usually an algorithm or data-structure issue — and measure again.

What does breakpoint() do?

It calls sys.breakpointhook(), which starts pdb at that line by default. The PYTHONBREAKPOINT environment variable can switch to another debugger or disable it (=0).

Testing & Tooling

Performance Tips

Fast Python comes from choosing the right algorithm and data structure, then letting C-implemented built-ins do the looping. Measure before and after every change.

Rule zero

“Premature optimisation is the root of all evil.” Write clear code first, profile (Debugging & Profiling), then optimise the hotspot the profiler shows you.

1. Algorithms and data structures first
Python
emails = [f"user{i}@x.com" for i in range(5_000)]
blocked = [f"user{i}@x.com" for i in range(0, 5_000, 7)]

# O(n·m): "in list" scans the whole list every time
slow = [e for e in emails if e not in blocked]

# O(n + m): build a set once, then O(1) lookups
blocked_set = set(blocked)
fast = [e for e in emails if e not in blocked_set]

print(slow == fast, len(fast))       # True 4285
Instead ofUseWhy
x in list inside a loopx in set / dictO(n) → O(1)
list.pop(0) / insert(0, x)collections.dequeO(n) → O(1) at both ends
Repeated min() of a changing listheapqO(n) → O(log n)
Linear search in sorted databisectO(n) → O(log n)
Recomputing the same resultsfunctools.cacheExponential → linear for DP-style recursion
Counting with a manual dictcollections.CounterWritten in C, clearer
2. Let built-ins do the looping
Python
nums = list(range(1, 100_001))

total = 0
for n in nums:                        # interpreted, one bytecode loop per item
    total += n * n

fast_total = sum(n * n for n in nums)   # sum() runs in C
print(total == fast_total)              # True

words = ["b", "a", "c"] * 3
print("".join(sorted(set(words))))      # abc   ← set, sorted, join: all C
print(max(nums), any(n > 99_999 for n in nums))   # 100000 True

Comprehensions

Faster than for + append — the loop runs with less bytecode overhead.

str.join

Build strings from a list of parts; += in a loop can copy repeatedly.

map with built-ins

list(map(int, parts)) avoids a Python-level lambda call per item.

Batch operations

list.extend, dict.update, set.update instead of item-by-item loops.

3. Reduce work inside hot loops
Python
import math

def distances_slow(points):
    result = []
    for x, y in points:
        result.append(math.sqrt(x * x + y * y))    # attribute lookups every iteration
    return result

def distances_fast(points):
    hypot = math.hypot                               # hoist the lookup into a local
    return [hypot(x, y) for x, y in points]          # comprehension + a specialised C function

pts = [(3, 4), (6, 8), (5, 12)]
print(distances_slow(pts) == distances_fast(pts), distances_fast(pts))   # True [5.0, 10.0, 13.0]
  • Local variables are faster than globals and attribute lookups (self.x, module.func) — hoist them out of tight loops.
  • Move invariant work (compiling a regex, building a set) outside the loop.
  • Avoid exceptions for normal control flow inside very hot loops — but try itself is free when nothing is raised (3.11+).
  • Use generators to stream large data instead of building huge lists: less memory means fewer cache misses and GC pauses.
4. Memory: slots, generators, arrays
Python
import sys
from array import array

class PointDict:
    def __init__(self, x, y):
        self.x, self.y = x, y

class PointSlots:
    __slots__ = ("x", "y")
    def __init__(self, x, y):
        self.x, self.y = x, y

a, b = PointDict(1, 2), PointSlots(1, 2)
print(hasattr(a, "__dict__"), hasattr(b, "__dict__"))   # True False

as_list = list(range(100_000))
as_array = array("i", range(100_000))                    # packed 4-byte C ints
print(sys.getsizeof(as_array) < sys.getsizeof(as_list))  # True
5. Cache and precompute
Python
from functools import cache

@cache
def ways(n):                  # count paths — exponential without the cache
    return 1 if n < 2 else ways(n - 1) + ways(n - 2)

print(ways(200))              # 453973694165307953197296969697410619233826
print(ways.cache_info().hits > 0)   # True
6. When pure Python isn't enough
ToolHow it helpsBest for
NumPy / pandasVectorised operations run in C over whole arraysNumeric and tabular data
multiprocessing / ProcessPoolExecutorUse every CPU coreCPU-bound, parallelisable work
asyncio / threadsOverlap waiting timeI/O-bound work
PyPyA JIT-compiled Python implementationLong-running pure-Python code
Cython / mypycCompile (typed) Python to C extensionsHot inner loops in libraries
NumbaJIT-compile numeric functions with a decoratorLoops over NumPy arrays
Rust / C extensions (PyO3)Write the hot core nativelyMaximum speed, bigger effort
Free speed

Upgrading Python is the cheapest optimisation: 3.11 was 10–60% faster than 3.10, and later releases continue the work (an experimental JIT arrived in 3.13).

Do

Profile, fix the top hotspot, re-measure, and keep a benchmark or test that proves the result is still correct.

Avoid

Micro-optimising code that runs once, or sacrificing readability for a 2% gain the profiler never pointed at.

Why is a list comprehension usually faster than a for loop with append?

The comprehension is compiled into a specialised loop that appends via a dedicated bytecode instead of looking up and calling the append method on each iteration, so there's less interpreter overhead per item.

How do you speed up CPU-bound Python code?

Better algorithm first; then vectorise with NumPy or move hot loops into C (built-ins, Cython, Numba); then parallelise across cores with multiprocessing, since threads don't run Python bytecode in parallel under the GIL.

PEP 8, Linters & Formatters

Consistent style makes code easier to read and review. PEP 8 defines the conventions; formatters and linters apply and enforce them automatically so humans can review logic instead of whitespace.

PEP 8 essentials
TopicConvention
Indentation4 spaces, never tabs
Line length79 in PEP 8; most teams use 88 (Black/Ruff) or 100
Functions, variables, modulessnake_case
Classes, exceptions, type variablesPascalCase (HttpError, UserProfile)
ConstantsUPPER_SNAKE_CASE at module level
Internal names_leading_underscore
Blank lines2 around top-level functions/classes, 1 between methods
ImportsOne per line, at the top: standard library → third-party → local, each group separated
Whitespacex = f(a, b), items[1:2], no spaces inside brackets, def f(x=1) without spaces around =
Comparisonsis None, if not items:, isinstance(x, int) instead of type(x) == int

import os                                  # 1. standard library
from collections import Counter

# import requests                          # 2. third-party
# from .models import User                 # 3. local package

MAX_RETRIES = 3


class RetryPolicy:
    """Decide whether a failed request should be retried."""

    def __init__(self, max_retries: int = MAX_RETRIES) -> None:
        self.max_retries = max_retries

    def should_retry(self, attempt: int, status: int) -> bool:
        return attempt < self.max_retries and status >= 500


policy = RetryPolicy()
print(policy.should_retry(1, 503), policy.should_retry(3, 503))   # True False
Docstrings (PEP 257)
Python
def transfer(src: str, dst: str, amount: float) -> str:
    """Move money between two accounts.

    Args:
        src: Account id to debit.
        dst: Account id to credit.
        amount: Positive amount in rupees.

    Returns:
        The transaction id.

    Raises:
        ValueError: If amount is not positive.
    """
    if amount <= 0:
        raise ValueError("amount must be positive")
    return f"txn:{src}->{dst}:{amount}"

print(transfer.__doc__.splitlines()[0])      # Move money between two accounts.
print(transfer("a1", "b2", 50))              # txn:a1->b2:50
Key point

A one-line summary in the imperative mood, a blank line, then details. Google style (above) and NumPy style are both common — pick one per project. Tools like Sphinx and mkdocstrings turn them into documentation.
